be the concatenation of these curves. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm sph}(W_{+},W_{-})
be the law of
(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
.
Note that for
W_{+}=W_{-}=\gamma^{2}-2
, we have
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm sph}(W_{+},W_{-})=\SLE^{p\rightleftharpoons q}_{\kappa}

for
(p,q)=(0,\infty)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\gamma=4/\sqrt{\kappa^{\prime}}
.
For
W\geq 2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}
, there is some constant
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathcal{M}^{\rm sph}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W}}\right)\otimes\text{\rm whole-plane}\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4W}{\gamma^{2}}-2}}\right)=c\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(W-(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2});\ell,\ell))d\ell.

(3.3)

For
W=W_{1}+W_{2}+4-\gamma^{2}
and
W_{1},W_{2}>0
, there is some constant
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathcal{M}^{\rm sph}_{2}(W)\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm sph}(W_{1},W_{2})=c\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(W_{1};\ell_{1},\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(W_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))d\ell_{1}d\ell_{2}.

(3.4)

Proof.

We first prove (
3.3
). According to Proposition
3.6
, the conformal welding of two independent forested lines gives a thin quantum disk with weight
2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}
decorated with an
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4;\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4}}\right)
. Therefore, the RHS of (
3.3
) is equal to (up to a multiplicative constant)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Weld}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}(W-(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2});\ell_{1},\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2};\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}}\right)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4;\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4}}\right)}}\right)d\ell_{1}d\ell_{2}.

Therefore (
3.3
) follows from Proposition
3.5
and
[
DMS21
, Theorem 1.17]
, which we recall:

Theorem

(
[
DMS21
, Theorem 1.17]
)
.

Let
\kappa=16/\kappa^{\prime}\in(2,4)
.
Sample
\eta_{0}
according to whole-plane
\SLE_{\kappa}(W-2)
on
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}
, and conditioned on
\eta_{0}
let
\eta_{1}
be chordal
\SLE_{\kappa}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W-4+\frac{\kappa}{2},-\frac{\kappa}{2}}}\right)
on
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\setminus\eta_{0}
. Then
(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
is a pair of GFF flow lines with an angle gap of
\pi
. Conditioned on
(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})
, in each connected component of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash(\eta_{0}\cap\eta_{1})
lying to the right of
\eta_{0}
and left of
\eta_{1}
sample an independent chordal
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4;\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{2}-4}}\right)
between the two boundary points hit by both
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
, and let
\eta
be the concatenation of these curves.
Then the marginal law of
\eta
is whole-plane
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\frac{4W}{\gamma^{2}}-2)
.

Now we prove (
3.4
). By Proposition
3.6
, for a sample from
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(W_{1};\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\times\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(W_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))d\ell_{1}
, conformally welding the boundary arcs having length
\ell_{1}
gives a curve-decorated quantum surface whose law is (up-to-constant)
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\rm f.d.}(W_{1}+W_{2}+2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2};\ell_{2},\ell_{2})\otimes\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho_{-};\rho_{+})\,d\ell_{2}
with
\rho_{\pm}=\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}(2-\gamma^{2}+W_{\pm})
. We then conformally weld the left and right boundary arcs and integrate over
\ell_{2}
. By (
3.3
) and the definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm sph}(W_{1},W_{2})
, the resulting curve-decorated quantum surface has law (up-to-constant)
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W_{1},W_{2})
, so (
3.4
) holds.
∎

From Proposition
3.7
we can obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.8
.

Fix distinct
p,q\in\mathbbm{C}
.
Let
(\mathbbm{C},h,p,q)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\rm sph}(\gamma^{2})
and independently sample
\eta
from
\SLE^{p\rightleftharpoons q}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Then the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta)
, viewed as a loop-decorated quantum surface, equals
C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{3}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell
for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.7
and the fact that
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\mathcal{M}^{\rm f.d.}_{2}(\gamma^{2}-2;\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
(see Definition
2.27
), the law of
(D,h,\eta,p,q)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals
C\iint_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}),\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}
.
Now the result follows by taking the pushforward
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\mapsto\ell=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
(i.e. forgetting the two marked points), similarly in the proof of
[
AHS23b
, Lemma 3.2]
.
∎

3.2
Proof of Theorem
3.3

The proof follows the argument of
[
AHS23b
]
, where the inputs are instead conformal welding results for forested quantum surfaces (developed in Section
3.2
).
We first need the following area-weighted variant of
\mathcal{M}^{\rm sph}_{2}(W)
in Definition
2.7
.

Definition 3.9
.

Fix
W>0
and let
(\mathcal{C},\phi,-\infty,+\infty)
be an embedding of a sample from the quantum-area-weighted measure
\mu_{\phi}(\mathcal{C})\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\rm sph}(W)
. Given
\phi
, sample
z
from the probability measure proportional to
\mu_{\phi}
. We write
\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\rm sph}(W)
for the law of the marked quantum surface
(\mathcal{C},\phi,-\infty,+\infty,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Let
M=\mathrm{QS}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\rm loop}
.
For a sample from
M
weighted by the quantum area times the square of the generalized quantum length, pick an arbitrary embedding
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta)
. Given
(h,\eta)
, we independently sample
p,q
from the probability measure proportional to the generalized quantum length and
r
from the probability measure proportional to the quantum area. Let
M_{3}
be the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta,p,q,r)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

Next, for fixed distinct points
p,q,r
in
\mathbbm{C}
, let
(\mathbbm{C},h,p,q,r)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\mathrm{sph}}(\gamma^{2})
and let
\eta
be independently sampled from
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{p\rightleftharpoons q}
. Let
\widetilde{M}_{3}
be the law of the decorated quantum surface
(\mathbbm{C},h,\eta,p,q,r)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Let
\widetilde{M}^{p,q,r}_{3}
be the law of the field-curve pair
(h,\eta)
, i.e.,
\widetilde{M}_{3}^{p,q,r}
is the law of an embedding of a sample from
\widetilde{M}_{3}
with marked points sent to
p,q,r
.

To show Theorem
3.3
, it suffices to show
M_{3}=C\widetilde{M}_{3}
for some constant
C>0
. Indeed, by Corollary
3.8
, if we deweight the total quantum area from
\widetilde{M}_{3}
and then forget its marked points, the resulting law is
C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell^{3}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell
. Further reweighting by
\ell^{-2}
we get
C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell
. On the other hand, applying the same weightings to
M_{3}
and forgetting the marked points yields
M
by definition. This gives
M=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathrm{GQD}(\ell))d\ell
, concluding the proof of Theorem
3.3
.

We will now show
M_{3}=C\widetilde{M}_{3}
. Theorem
2.13
states that
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\mathrm{QS}=C\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}
, and hence
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes M=C\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}\times\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
by the conformal invariance of
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
. Building on this,
we have the following counterpart of
[
AHS23b
, Lemma 3.5]
(which considers
\kappa\in(0,4)
) via exactly the same argument:

\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes M_{3}=C|p-q|^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},p),(\frac{2}{\gamma},q),(\gamma,r)}(d\phi)\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{p\rightleftharpoons q}(d\eta)d^{2}pd^{2}qd^{2}r.

(3.5)

Instead of repeating the argument, we just point out the two minor modifications needed. The right hand side of
[
AHS23b
, Lemma 3.5]
is
C|p-q|^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{\gamma}{2},p),(\frac{\gamma}{2},q),(\gamma,r)}(d\phi)\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{p\rightleftharpoons q}(d\eta)d^{2}pd^{2}qd^{2}r
. Since the generalized quantum length of
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
is a
\frac{2}{\gamma}
-GMC (the quantum length of
\SLE_{\kappa}
is a
\frac{\gamma}{2}
-GMC), the insertions of the Liouville field in (
3.5
) are
\frac{2}{\gamma}
rather than
\frac{\gamma}{2}
. Further, the polynomial term in (
3.5
) is
|p-q|^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}
rather than
|p-q|^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-2}
; this corresponds to having
|p-q|^{-2(1-\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{8})}
rather than
|p-q|^{-2(1-\frac{\kappa}{8})}
in (
3.1
). Other than these, the proof of (
3.5
) is identical to that of
[
AHS23b
, Lemma 3.5]
.

On the other hand, following the proof of
[
AHS23b
, Lemma 3.8]
, we have

\widetilde{M}^{p,q,r}_{3}=C|p-q|^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-2}|(p-q)(q-r)(p-r)|^{2}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},p),(\frac{2}{\gamma},q),(\gamma,r)}(d\phi)\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{p\rightleftharpoons q}(d\eta).

(3.6)

Indeed, from the definition of
\widetilde{M}^{p,q,r}_{3}
one can first find that
\widetilde{M}_{3}^{0,1,-1}=C\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\frac{2}{\gamma},0),(\frac{2}{\gamma},1),(\gamma,r)}(d\phi)\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{0\rightleftharpoons 1}(d\eta)
. Then let
f\in{\rm conf}(\widehat{C})
be that
(0,1,-1)\mapsto(p,q,r)
and use the transformation law
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},f(z_{i}))_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}|f^{\prime}(z_{i})|^{-2\Delta_{\alpha_{i}}}f_{*}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha_{i},z_{i})_{i}}
with
\Delta_{\alpha}:=\frac{\alpha}{2}(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2})
(see e.g.
[
AHS23b
, Proposition 2.9]
for more details), one obtains (
3.6
).

Finally recall that
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\widetilde{M}_{3}=C\widetilde{M}^{p,q,r}_{3}|(p-q)(q-r)(r-p)|^{-2}d^{2}pd^{2}qd^{2}r
.
Hence combining (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) we conclude
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes M_{3}=C\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}}\ltimes\widetilde{M}_{3}
for some constant
C
; and then we deduce
M_{3}=C\widetilde{M}_{3}
by disintegration (see
[
AHS23b
, Section 3.1]
for more details).
∎

4
Backgrounds and preliminary results on CLE coupled with LQG

In this section, we review existing results on CLE coupled with independent quantum disks or generalized quantum disks, where the CLE and LQG parameters are related by
\kappa\in\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\}
.
Some of these results were implicitly obtained and others were sketched in prior literature; we will give details or alternative derivations.

4.1
The independent coupling of LQG and simple CLE

Let
\gamma\in(\sqrt{8/3},2)
and
\kappa=\gamma^{2}
.
Suppose that
(D,h)
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}
, where
D
is a bounded domain. Let
\Gamma
be a (non-nested)
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
D
that is independent of
h
.
Then we call the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
a
\CLE_{\kappa}
decorated quantum disk and
denote its law by
\mathrm{QD}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. By the conformal invariance of
\CLE_{\kappa}
, the measure
\mathrm{QD}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}

does not depend on the choice of embedding of
(D,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

Fix
a>0
. Recall the probability measure
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}=|\mathrm{QD}(a)|^{-1}\mathrm{QD}(a)
that corresponds to the quantum disk with boundary length
a
.
We define the probability measure
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
in the same way as
\mathrm{QD}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
with
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}
in place of
\mathrm{QD}
.
We can similarly define measures such as
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
and
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Given a loop
\eta
in
\Gamma
, let
D_{\eta}
be the bounded component of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta
, namely the region encircled by
\eta
.
A loop
\eta\in\Gamma
is called
outermost
if it is not contained in any
D_{\eta^{\prime}}
for
\eta^{\prime}\in\Gamma
.
Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of the quantum lengths of the outermost loops of
\Gamma
listed in non-increasing order.
Two crucial inputs to our paper are the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and the conditional law of the quantum surfaces encircled by the outermost loops
conditioned on
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
. We summarize them as the two propositions below.

Proposition 4.1

(
[
MSW20
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
)
.

Set
{\beta}:=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}\in(\frac{3}{2},2)
. Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a
{\beta}
-stable Lévy process
whose Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
, so that it has no downward jumps. We denote its law by
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Let
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the sequence of the sizes of the upward jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a}]
sorted in decreasing order.
Then the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
defined right above equals that of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
under the reweighted probability
\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}
.

It was pointed out at the end of
[
CCM20
, Section 1]
that Proposition
4.1
can be extracted from
[
MSW20
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
. The reason is that
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
are
two ways of describing the scaling limit of the outermost loop lengths of an
O(n)
-loop-decorated planar map model.
The former follows from
[
BBCK18
,
MSW20
]
and the latter follows from
[
CCM20
]
.
We explain this in more detail in Section
4.3
.

We also need the following quantum zipper result for a CLE outermost loop on the quantum disk.
Recall the measure
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)
corresponds to the quantum disk with one interior marked point and boundary length
a
.
Recall the probability measure
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
defined at the beginning of this subsection.
For a
\CLE_{\kappa}
sample
\Gamma
on
D
and a domain
U\subset D
,
we write
\Gamma|_{U}
for the subset of loops that lie in
U
.

Proposition 4.2
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
.
Let
D_{\eta}
and
A_{\eta}
be the two connected components of
D\setminus\eta
where
z\in D_{\eta}
.
Let
\ell_{h}
be the quantum boundary length measure on
\eta
.
Conditioning on
(h,\Gamma,\eta,z)
, let
w
be a point on
\eta
sampled from the probability measure proportional to
\ell_{h}
.
Now consider the joint distribution of
(D,h,\eta,z,w)
.
Then conditioning on
\ell_{h}(\eta)
, the decorated quantum surfaces
(D_{\eta},h,z,w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
(A_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{A_{\eta}},w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}

are conditionally independent, and the conditional law of
(D_{\eta},h,z,w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta))^{\#}
.

Proposition
4.2
is implicitly proved in
[
MSW20
]
, see Section
4.4
for more details.

4.2
The independent coupling of LQG and non-simple CLE

Let
\gamma\in(\sqrt{2},2)
and
\kappa=16/\gamma^{2}
.
Suppose that
(D,h)
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}
, where
D
is a bounded domain. Let
\Gamma
be the union of independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
’s in each connected component of
\mathrm{int}(D)
.
Then we call the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
a
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated quantum disk and
denote its law by
\mathrm{GQD}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
.

As the loops in
\Gamma
are non-simple, we need to give the following definitions.
We say a loop
\eta

surrounds
a point
z
if
z\not\in\eta
and
\eta
has a nonzero winding number with respect to
z
. We call a loop
\eta\in\Gamma

outermost
if no point of
\eta
is surrounded by any loop in
\Gamma
.
Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the collection of the quantum natural time of the outermost loops of
\Gamma
listed in non-increasing order.

We first state the
\kappa\in(4,8)
analog of Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
MSW21
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
)
.

Set
{\beta}:=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
. Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a
{\beta}
-stable Lévy process
whose Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
, so that it has no downward jumps. We denote its law by
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Let
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the sequence of the sizes of the upward jumps of
\zeta
on
[0,\tau_{-a}]
sorted in decreasing order.
Then the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
defined right above equals that of
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
under the reweighted probability
\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}}{\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}
.

In Section
4.3
we will explain how Proposition
4.3
follows from
[
MSW21
,
BBCK18
,
CCM20
]
. The argument is the same as that of Proposition
4.1
.

Now, we state the
\kappa\in(4,8)
analog of Proposition
4.2
. The statement is more complicated due to the nontrivial topologies of the random surfaces involved.
Let
\mathrm{Ann}
denote the set of quantum surfaces with the annulus topology having distinguished inner and outer boundaries and decorated by a countable collection of loops. Concretely,
\mathrm{Ann}
is the set of equivalence classes
(A,h,\Gamma,\partial_{\mathrm{out}}A,\partial_{\mathrm{in}}A)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
where
A\subset\mathbbm{C}
has the annulus topology,
\partial_{\mathrm{out}}A
and
\partial_{\mathrm{in}}A
are the boundary components of
A
,
h
is a distribution on
A
,
\Gamma
is a collection of loops on
A
, and
\sim_{\gamma}
identifies pairs of tuples if there exists a conformal map identifying the domains, boundaries and loops, which also relates the fields via the LQG coordinate change (
2.2
). Similarly, let
\mathrm{Ann}^{\prime}
denote the set of beaded quantum surfaces arising from a countable collection of loop-ensemble-decorated two-pointed disk-topology quantum surfaces endowed with a cyclic ordering, and say its inner (resp. outer) boundary is the union of the left (resp. right) boundary arcs of the two-pointed quantum surfaces. The definition of
\mathrm{Ann}^{\prime}
is analogous to that of a thin quantum disk, except the ordering is cyclic. See Figure
1
.

Figure 1:

Left:
An element of
\mathrm{Ann}
.
Middle
: An element of
\mathrm{Ann}^{\prime}
.
Right:
A sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#}
. Note that both configurations are possible.

As explained below Proposition
2.31
, forested quantum surfaces arise from adding a Poisson point process of generalized quantum disks to the boundary of a quantum surface. If we further sample in each connected component of the added generalized quantum disks an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, we call it
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated foresting.

Proposition 4.4
.

There exists a measure
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
on
\mathrm{Ann}\cup\mathrm{Ann}^{\prime}
such that, if
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#}
denotes the law of a sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
with
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated foresting conditioned on having outer (resp. inner) generalized boundary length
a
(resp.
b
), then the following holds:

For
a,b>0
, the law of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
conditioned on the length of the outermost loop surrounding the marked bulk point being
b
is
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#},\mathrm{GQD}(b)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})
.

Remark 4.5
.

In Proposition
4.4
, because
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#}
is defined in terms of
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
, by definition
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#}
is the law of a forested quantum surface (i.e., the generalized quantum disks added to the boundary arise from a Poisson point process according to quantum length). Thus, the following is an informal way of rephrasing Proposition
4.4
:

For a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, condition on the length of the outermost loop
\eta
around the bulk point being
b
. Then, cutting by
\eta
gives a pair of conditionally independent forested quantum surfaces
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
corresponding to the regions outside and inside
\eta
respectively; the conditional law of
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathrm{GQD}(b)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, and we denote the conditional law of
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#}
. Furthermore, if we condition on
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
, the initial surface agrees in law with the uniform conformal welding of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
.

We also note that the proof of Proposition
4.4
yields an explicit description of
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
via conformal weldings of
forested quantum triangles
.

Proposition
4.4
is implicitly proved in
[
MSW21
]
(in the same way that Proposition
4.2
is implicitly proved in
[
MSW20
]
). The explanation would be more complicated than that of Proposition
4.2
, so we will instead give an alternative proof in Section
4.5
based on
[
AHSY23
]
.

4.3
Scaling limit of O(n)-loop-decorated planar maps: Proofs of Propositions
4.1
and
4.3

In this section we explain why Propositions
4.1
and
4.3
hold; since both have the same proof we focus on the former, and briefly discuss the latter at the end.
The claim that
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
agree in law holds because they give two descriptions of the scaling limit of the same discrete model: loop lengths in the
O(n)
-loop-decorated quadrangulation. This was pointed out at the end of Section 1 of
[
CCM20
]
.
We give a more detailed justification by assembling results in
[
CCM20
,
BBCK18
,
MSW20
]
.

We recall the loop-decorated quadrangulation from
[
CCM20
]
.
A quadrangulation with boundary is a planar map where each face has degree four except a distinguished face which we call the external face. (Others faces are called internal faces.)
The degree of the external face is called the perimeter.
A (rigid) loop configuration on a quadrangulation with a boundary is a set of disjoint undirected simple closed paths in the dual map which do not visit the external face, and with
the additional constraint that when a loop visits a face of q it must cross it through opposite edges.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{p}
be the set of pairs
(\mathbf{q},\Gamma)
such that
\mathbf{q}
is a quadrangulation with boundary whose perimeter is
2p
, and
\Gamma
is a loop configuration on
\mathbf{q}
. Similar as for CLE, for each
\Gamma
on
\mathbf{q}
, there is a collection of outermost loops whose are not surrounded by any other loop in
\Gamma
.

We now recall a scaling limit result in
[
CCM20
]
.
Recall
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}
, let
n\in(0,2)
be such that
\beta=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\arccos(n/2)
.
For each
(\mathbf{q},\Gamma)\in\mathcal{O}_{p}
, we let
|\mathbf{q}|
be the number of faces of
\mathbf{q}
, let
|\Gamma|
be the total length of all loops of
\Gamma
, and let
\#\Gamma
be the number of loops in
\Gamma
. For
h>0,g>0
, assign weight
w(\mathbf{q},\Gamma)=g^{|\mathbf{q}|-|\Gamma|}h^{|\Gamma|}n^{\#\Gamma}
to
(\mathbf{q},\Gamma)
.
For some choices of
(g,h)
, this gives a finite measure on
\mathcal{O}_{p}
which can be normalized into a probability measure.
Let
\mathfrak{M}_{p}
be a sample from this measure. Let
L_{1}^{p}\geq L_{2}^{p}\geq\dots
be the lengths of the outermost loops of
\mathfrak{M}_{p}
in decreasing order.

Proposition 4.6

(
[
CCM20
, Proposition 3]
)
.

There exists
(g,h)
such that
as
p\to\infty
, the sequence
(\frac{a}{2p}L_{i}^{p})_{i\geq 1}
converges in law to
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, where
(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and
a>0
are as in Proposition
4.1
.

We refer to
[
CCM20
, Definition 1]
for a description of
(g,h)
such that Proposition
4.6
holds.
In the rest of the section we explain why the following proposition follows from
[
BBCK18
,
MSW20
]
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For
(g,h)
satisfying Proposition
4.6
,
(\frac{a}{2p}L_{i}^{p})_{i\geq 1}
converges in law to
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
as defined in Proposition
4.1
.

We first describe
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
in terms of a growth fragmentation process considered in
[
MSW20
]
and
[
BBCK18
]
.
We will not give the full description of the growth fragmentation but only provide enough information to specify the law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
.
Our presentation is based on the treatment in
[
BBCK18
]
.
The description of growth fragmentation process in
[
MSW20
]
is different in appearance.
But as explained in
[
MSW20
, Remark 6.2]
they correspond to the same process.

For
\theta=\frac{4}{\kappa}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
, let
\nu_{\theta}
be the measure on
(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
defined by

\nu_{\theta}(dx)=\frac{\Gamma(\theta+1)}{\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1_{1/2<x<1}}{(x(1-x))^{\theta+1}}+\sin(\pi(\theta-\frac{1}{2}))\frac{1_{x>1}}{(x(x-1))^{\theta+1}}}}\right)dx.

(4.1)

Let
\Lambda_{\theta}
be the pushforward of
\nu_{\theta}
under the map
x\mapsto\log x
.
For
\lambda>0
, let

\Psi_{\theta}(\lambda)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\Gamma(2-\theta)}{2\Gamma(2-2\theta)\sin(\pi\theta)}+\frac{\Gamma(\theta+1)B_{\frac{1}{2}}(-\theta,2-\theta)}{\pi}}}\right)\lambda+\int_{\mathbbm{R}}(e^{\lambda y}-1+\lambda(1-e^{y}))\Lambda_{\theta}(dy),

where
B_{\frac{1}{2}}(a,b)=\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}\,dt
is the incomplete Beta function; see
[
BBCK18
, (28)]
. By

[
BBCK18
, Theorem 5.1, Proposition 5.2]
, there is a real-valued Lévy process
(\xi(t))_{t\geq 0}
whose law is described by

\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\xi(t)}]=e^{t\Psi_{\theta}(\lambda)}
for all
\lambda,t>0
.
For
t\geq 0
, let
\tau_{t}=\inf\{r\geq 0\>:\>\int_{0}^{r}e^{\theta\xi(s)}\,ds\geq t\}
. Then
\tau_{t}
a.s. reaches
\infty
in finite time.
For
a>0
, define

Y_{t}^{a}:=a\exp(\xi(\tau_{ta^{-\theta}}))\quad\text{for }t\geq 0,

with the convention that
\xi(+\infty)=-\infty
. Then
(Y_{t}^{a})_{t\geq 0}
is nonnegative Markov process , with initial value
Y_{0}^{a}=a
and a.s. hits
0
in finite time.
Since
\nu_{\theta}
is supported on
(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
, the downward jumps
y\to y-\ell
of
(Y_{t}^{a})_{t\geq 0}
satisfy
\ell<\frac{1}{2}y
.

We now relate
(Y_{t}^{a})_{t\geq 0}
to the CPI on quantum disks reviewed in Section
4.4
. Suppose
(D,h,\Gamma,x,y)
are as in Proposition
4.10
such that the law of
(D,h,\Gamma,x)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
The chordal CPI from
x
to
y
can be viewed as an exploration of the carpet of
\Gamma
such that
at any splitting time it goes into the domain with the target
y
on its boundary. Namely, it enters
D_{t}
instead of
\mathcal{B}_{t}
to continue.
We can also alter the exploration rule at these splitting times, each of which defines a variant of CPI and corresponds to a branch in the so-called CPI branching exploration tree rooted at
x
. One particular variant of CPI is such that at any splitting time it enters the domain with the largest quantum boundary length.
In the terminology of
[
MSW20
]
, this is called the CPI with (
q=\infty
)-exploration mechanism.

Let
\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{t}
be the boundary length of the to-be-explored region at time
t
CPI with (
q=\infty
)-exploration mechanism. Then by
[
MSW20
, Theorem 1.2]
,
for some constant
c>0
not depending on
a
, we have
(\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{ct})_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(Y^{a}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Moreover, the upward jumps in
\widetilde{Y}_{t}
correspond to times when the CPI discovers a loop, and downward jumps in
\widetilde{Y}_{t}
correspond to times when the CPI splits the to-be-explored region into two.
Iteratively applying this fact we get
the following description the outermost loop lengths is as in Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 4.8

(
[
MSW20
]
)
.

The lengths
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
agree in law with
(L_{i})_{i\geq 1}
sampled as follows.
First sample
(Y^{a}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, and let
U_{1}
and
D_{1}
be the sets of the sizes of upward and downward jumps of
(Y^{a}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Given
D_{1}
, sample a collection of independent processes
S_{2}=\{(Y^{x}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\>:\>x\in D_{1}\}
, and let
U_{2}
and
D_{2}
be the sets of the sizes of all upward and downward jumps of processes in
S_{2}
. Iteratively define
S_{i},U_{i},D_{i}
for all
i
, and finally set
U=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}U_{i}
. Finally, rank the elements of
U
as
L_{1}\geq L_{2}\geq\cdots
.

Proof.

The quantum lengths of loops discovered by CPI with (
q=\infty
)-exploration mechanism
correspond to the sizes of the upward jumps in
\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{t}
, which has the same law as the upward jumps in
Y^{a}_{t}
.
The analogous Markov properties in Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
still hold for this CPI.
Now we continue this exploration mechanism to explore the rest of CLE carpet. Iteratively applying this relation the quantum length of the discovered loops and the upward jumps, we get Proposition
4.8
.
∎

We now explain how Proposition
4.7
follows from the scaling limit results for
\mathfrak{M}_{p}
proved in
[
BBCK18
, Section 6]
.

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Let
{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{p}}
be the planar map obtained from
\mathfrak{M}_{p}
by removing all the regions surrounded by outermost loops on
\mathfrak{M}_{p}
.
For
(g,h)
satisfying Proposition
4.6
,
{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{p}}
is the so-called critical non-generic Boltzmann planar map considered in
[
BBCK18
, Section 6]
. The map
{\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{p}}
is the discrete analog of the CLE carpet on the LQG background. The discrete analog of CPI exploration for CLE carpet is considered in
[
BBCK18
, Section 6.3]
which is called the branching peeling exploration. The exact analog of the CPI with (
q=\infty
)-exploration mechanism is considered in
[
BBCK18
, Section 6.4]
, where the exploration is always towards the component with the largest perimeter when there is splitting.
It was shown in
[
BBCK18
, Proposition 6.6]
that the rescaled lengths of the loops discovered by this peeling process converge in law to
the sizes of the upward jumps in
Y^{a}_{t}
. Iterating the exploration in both discrete and continuum, we get the desired convergence.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Combining Propositions
4.6
and
4.7
, we conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

The argument is exactly the same as that of Proposition
4.1
. All results taken from
[
CCM20
]
and
[
BBCK18
]
still hold for
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
and
\theta=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. The results from
[
MSW20
]
each have non-simple CLE counterparts in
[
MSW21
]
; in particular, to see
(\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{ct})_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(Y^{a}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
[
MSW21
, Theorem 5.1]
gives
(\widetilde{Y}^{a}_{ct})_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(Y^{a}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
when
\sin(\pi(\theta-\frac{1}{2}))
in (
4.1
) is replaced by a quantity they call
A_{+}(p)/A_{-}(p)
, and
[
MSW21
, Remark 5.2]
identifies this quantity as
-\cos(\pi\theta)
(
=\sin(\pi(\theta-\frac{1}{2}))
).
∎

4.4
Proof of Proposition
4.2

We now explain how Proposition
4.2
follows from
[
MSW20
]
. It reduces to the following:

Proposition 4.9
.

In the setting of Proposition
4.2
, we can find a random point
p\in\eta
such that, conditioning on
(A_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{A_{\eta}},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
,
the conditional law of
(D_{\eta},h,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta))^{\#}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
given Proposition
4.9
.

Suppose
(h,\Gamma,p,z)
satisfies Proposition
4.9
.
Conditioning on
(h,\Gamma,p,z)
, let
U
be a uniform random variable on
(0,1)
. Let
w
be the point of
\eta
such that the counterclockwise arc on
\eta
from
p
to
w

is of
\ell_{h}
-length
U\ell_{h}(\eta)
. By definition,
w
is sampled from the probability measure proportional to
\ell_{h}(\eta)
. By Proposition
4.9
and the re-rooting invariance of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta))
, conditioning on
(A_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{A_{\eta}},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
U
, the conditional law of
(D_{\eta},h,z,w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta))
. Since
(A_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{A_{\eta}},w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is determined by
(A_{\eta},h,\Gamma|_{A_{\eta}},p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
U
, we are done.
∎

To find the desired
p
in Proposition
4.9
, we use the

conformal percolation interface
(CPI) within a CLE carpet introduced by Miller, Sheffield and Werner
[
MSW17
]
.
Suppose
\Gamma
is a
\CLE_{\kappa}
on a Jordan domain
D
(i.e.
\partial D
is a simple curve) for some
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
.
Given two boundary points
x,y
, a (chordal) CPI for
\Gamma
from
x
to
y
is a random curve from
x
to
y

coupled with
\Gamma
that does not enter the interior of any region surrounded by a loop of
\Gamma
(but it can touch the loops).
We also need to specify how a CPI proceeds upon hitting a loop of
\Gamma
on its way from
x
to
y
. We require that it always leaves the loop to its right.
In the terminology of
[
MSW17
]
, this corresponds to CPI with
\beta=1
.
The marginal law of a CPI is
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
on
D
from
x
to
y
where
\kappa^{\prime}=16/\kappa

[
MSW17
, Theorem 7.7 (ii)]
and the force point is on the right of
x
. In particular, a CPI is a non-simple curve.
Intuitively, the chordal CPI describes the chordal interface of a percolation on a CLE carpet.
It is characterized by certain conformal invariance and Markov properties which are consistent with this intuition; see
[
MSW17
, Definition 2.1]
.
We will not review the full details but will rely on an analogous Markov property that CPI satisfies on a quantum disk background.
This was established in
[
MSW20
]
, which we review now.

Fix
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
. For
L_{0},R_{0}>0
,
suppose
(D,h,\Gamma,x)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}^{\#}(L_{0}+R_{0})\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Let
y
be the point on
\partial D
such that the quantum length of the counterclockwise arc from
x
to
y
is
R_{0}
.
Conditioning on
(h,\Gamma,x,y)
, sample a CPI
\eta^{\prime}
within the carpet of
\Gamma
from
x
to
y
. Since the law of
\eta^{\prime}
is a
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
,
there is a quantum natural time parametrization for
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to
h

[
DMS21
, Section 6]
, which we use throughout.
Under this parametrization,
\eta^{\prime}
has a finite duration
T
.
For a fixed time
t>0
, on the event that
t\leq T
, let
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{t}
be the union of
\eta^{\prime}[0,t]
and all the loops of
\Gamma

touched by
\eta^{\prime}[0,t]
. If
t<T
, let
D_{t}
be the simply connected component of
D\setminus\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{t}
which contains
y
on its boundary.
For a fixed
t
, both
D_{t}
and the interior of
D\setminus D_{t}
are Jordan domains a.s. We write the interior
D\setminus D_{t}
as
U_{t}
. The interface between
D_{t}
and
U_{t}
are
\SLE_{\kappa}
types curves, on which there is a well-defined quantum length measure.

Figure 2:
We color
D_{t}
blue and
U_{t}
green.
(a):
At all but countably many times
t
, we have
D_{t}=D_{t^{-}}
. To simplify (b)–(d) we omit their loops.
(b)
: A loop discovery time.
(c), (d):
A splitting time. At times when the left boundary length
L_{t}
has a downward jump, there are two possible topologies; we do not illustrate the similar cases when the split is to the right.

The following Markov property of CPI on quantum disks was proved in
[
MSW20
]
, although it was not explicitly stated.

Proposition 4.10

(
[
MSW20
]
)
.

For a fixed
t>0
, on the even that
t<T
, let
L_{t}
and
R_{t}
be the quantum lengths of the clockwise and counterclockwise arcs from
\eta^{\prime}(t)
to
y
of
\partial D_{t}
.
Conditioning on the decorated quantum surface
(U_{t},h,\Gamma|_{U_{t}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
(L_{t},R_{t})
,
the conditional law of

(D_{t},h,\Gamma|_{D_{t}},y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(L_{t}+R_{t})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.

Proof.

This proposition is essentially
[
MSW20
, Proposition 5.1]
, except that we condition on more information than they explicitly stated. But the argument carries over directly to our setting.
∎

The point
p
in Proposition
4.9
that we will find is a point where a CPI hits a loop. Therefore we need a stronger variant of the Markov property in Proposition
4.10
at certain random times which we now define.
For each
t\in(0,T)
, let
D_{t^{-}}
be the interior of
\cap_{s<t}D_{s}
.
According to
[
MSW20
]
, for each fixed time
t
, on the event that
t<T
, almost surely
D_{t^{-}}=D_{t}
; see Figure
2
(a).
But there exist countably many times where
D_{t^{-}}\neq D_{t}
. In this case, there are two scenarios:

1.

The point
\eta^{\prime}(t)
is on a loop of
\Gamma
. In this case the interior of
D_{t^{-}}\setminus D_{t}
is the Jordan domain enclosed by the this loop. But
D_{t}
is not a Jordan domain since
\eta^{\prime}(t)
corresponds to two points on
\partial D_{t}
.
See Figure
2
(b). We call
t
a loop discovery time.

2.

The point
\eta^{\prime}(t)
is not on a loop of
\Gamma
. In this case, both
D_{t}
and
D_{t^{-}}\setminus D_{t}
are Jordan domains, and their boundaries intersect at the single point
\eta^{\prime}(t)
.
See Figure
2
(c)–(d). We call
t
a splitting time.

In both cases, we let
\mathcal{B}_{t}
be the interior of
D_{t^{-}}\setminus D_{t}
and
U_{t^{-}}
be the interior of
D\setminus D_{t^{-}}
. Then
\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}\setminus\partial D
is an
\SLE_{\kappa}
type curve. By definition,
\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}
is a loop in
\Gamma
if and only if
t
is a loop discovery time.
Recall
(L_{t},R_{t})
from Proposition
4.10
. If
t
if it is a loop discovery time, then
R_{t}
has an upward jump. If
t
is a splitting time, then either
L_{t}
or
R_{t}
has a downward jump. In both cases, the size of the jump equals the quantum length of
\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}
, which we denote by
X_{t}
.
We now state the stronger version of Proposition
4.10
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and a positive integer
n
. Let
\tau
be the
n
-th time such that
D_{t^{-}}\neq D_{t}
and the quantum length
X_{t}
of

\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}
is larger than
\varepsilon
. If this time never occurs, set
\tau=\infty
. Conditioning on
\tau<\infty
, the decorated quantum surface
(U_{\tau^{-}},h,\Gamma|_{U_{\tau^{-}}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, the indicator
1_{\{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\textrm{ is a loop}\}}
, and the quantum lengths
X_{\tau}
of
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
and
L_{\tau},R_{\tau}
of the two arcs on
D_{\tau}
, the conditional law of
(D_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{D_{\tau}},y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is given by independent samples from
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(L_{\tau}+R_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
and
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(X_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
, respectively.

Proof.

For a fixed
t>0
, on the event that
t\leq T
, consider the ordered collection of decorated quantum surfaces

\{(\mathcal{B}_{s},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{s}},\eta^{\prime}(s))/{\sim_{\gamma}}:s\leq t\textrm{ and }D_{s^{-}}\neq D_{s}\}
.
It was proved in
[
MSW20
]
that conditioning on
(D_{t},h,\Gamma|_{D_{t}},\eta^{\prime}(t),y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, and the ordered information of the quantum lengths of their boundaries and whether their times are loop discovery or splitting,
the conditional law of these decorated quantum surfaces are independent
\CLE_{\kappa}
decorated quantum disks with given boundary lengths.
To see why this assertion follows from
[
MSW20
]
, we note that Propositions 3.1 and 3.5 of
[
MSW20
]
yield the corresponding assertion for the analogous case of CLE on the quantum half-plane. The pinching argument of
[
MSW20
, Proposition 5.1]
then gives this assertion.

We claim that conditioning on
\tau<\infty
,
(U_{\tau^{-}},h,\Gamma|_{U_{\tau^{-}}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
,
1_{\{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\textrm{ is a loop}\}}
, and
X_{\tau}
,
L_{\tau},R_{\tau}
, the conditional law of
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(X_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. Fix a large
k>0
. Let
s_{k}
be the largest integer multiple of
2^{-k}
smaller than
\tau
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{t}=(U_{t},h,\Gamma|_{U_{t}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,t]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{t}=(D_{t},h|_{D_{t}},\Gamma|_{D_{t}},y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
For a fixed
j
, by Proposition
4.10
, conditioning on
\mathcal{U}_{j2^{-k}}
and
(L_{j2^{-k}},R_{j2^{-k}})
,
the conditional law of
\mathcal{D}_{j2^{-k}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(L_{j2^{-k}}+R_{j2^{-k}})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Note that
\{s_{k}=j2^{-k}\}
is determined by
\mathcal{U}_{j2^{-k}}
and the quantum lengths of the boundaries of elements in

\{(\mathcal{B}_{s},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{s}},\eta^{\prime}(s))/{\sim_{\gamma}}:j2^{-k}\leq s\leq(j+1)2^{-k}\textrm{ and }D_{s^{-}}\neq D_{s}\}
.
Applying the assertion of the first paragraph to
\mathcal{D}_{j2^{-k}}
with
T=2^{-k}
,
we see that conditioning on
\tau<\infty
,
\mathcal{U}_{s_{k}}
,
\{s_{k}=j2^{-k}\}
,
1_{\{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\textrm{ is a loop}\}}
,
X_{\tau}
,

L_{s_{k}+2^{-k}}
and
R_{s_{k}+2^{-k}}
,
the conditional law of
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(X_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. Varying
j
, we can remove the condition
\{s_{k}=j2^{-k}\}
. Since almost surely

\mathcal{U}_{s_{k}}\rightarrow(U_{\tau^{-}},h,\Gamma|_{U_{\tau^{-}}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
(L_{s_{k}+2^{-k}},R_{s_{k}+2^{-k}})\rightarrow(L_{\tau},R_{\tau})
as
k\to\infty
, we have proved the desired claim.

It remains to show that conditioning on
\tau<\infty
,
(U_{\tau^{-}},h,\Gamma|_{U_{\tau^{-}}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
,
1_{\{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}\textrm{ is a loop}\}}
,
X_{\tau}
,
L_{\tau},R_{\tau}
and
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, the conditional law of
(D_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{D_{\tau}},y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(L_{\tau}+R_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. This follows from a similar but easier argument: we consider the smallest multiple of
2^{-k}
larger than
\tau
and use the Markov property in Proposition
4.10
at this time.
We omit the details. ∎

Proof of Proposition
4.9
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,x)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
.
Now we reweight
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
by
\mu_{h}(D)
and sample a point
z
according to the probability measure proportional to
\mu_{h}
.
This way, the law of
(D,h,\Gamma,z,x)
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
as in Propositions
4.2
and
4.9
. Let
y
be the point on
\partial D
such that both the two arcs between
x
to
y
have quantum length
a/2
, and sample a CPI
\eta^{\prime}
from
x
to
y
, parametrized by quantum natural time. Let
t_{0}
be the time such
z\in\mathcal{B}_{t_{0}}
and set
p_{0}=\eta^{\prime}(t_{0})
.

Consider
\tau
and
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
as defined in Proposition
4.11
with this choice of
(D,h,\Gamma,x,y)
.
Then on the event that
t_{0}=\tau
, namely
z\in\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
, conditioning on
(U_{\tau^{-}},h,\Gamma|_{U_{\tau^{-}}},\eta^{\prime}|_{[0,\tau]})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
,
1_{\{\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}\textrm{ is a loop}\}}
, the quantum length
X_{\tau}
of
\partial\mathcal{B}_{\tau}

and
(D_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{D_{\tau}},y)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, the conditional law of

(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},z,\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(X_{\tau})^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
, where we have
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}
instead of
\mathrm{QD}_{0,1}
because of area weighting.
This means that conditioning on the quantum intrinsic information on
D\setminus\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
,
the conditional law of
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},z,\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is a CLE decorated marked quantum disk with given boundary length.
By varying
\varepsilon
and
n
in Proposition
4.11
, the same holds with
(\mathcal{B}_{\tau},h|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{\tau}},z,\eta^{\prime}(\tau))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
replaced by

(\mathcal{B}_{t_{0}},h,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{B}_{t_{0}}},z,\eta^{\prime}(t_{0}))/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

If
t_{0}
is a loop discovery time, then
\partial\mathcal{B}_{t_{0}}
is the loop
\eta
surrounding
z
and
p_{0}=\eta^{\prime}(t_{0})
is the desired point we need for Proposition
4.9
. Otherwise, we set
D_{1}=\mathcal{B}_{t_{0}}
and construct
t_{1}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t_{1}}
and
p_{1}
as above with
(D,h,\Gamma,x)
replaced by
(D_{1},h,\Gamma|_{D_{1}},p_{0})
. If
p_{1}\in\eta
then we are done by setting
p=p_{1}
. Otherwise, we iterate this procedure and construct
p_{2},p_{3},\cdots
.
We claim that there must be a finite
k
such that
p_{k}\in\eta
, hence we can set
p=p_{k}
and conclude the proof.

To see the finiteness of the iteration, recall the set
(\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{t})_{t\geq[0,T]}
defined from a CPI
\eta^{\prime}
. We now require that once
\eta^{\prime}
hits a loop, it first finishes tracing the loop counterclockwise and then proceeds in its own track. This turns
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}_{T}
into the trace of a non-self-crossing curve sharing the same endpoints as
\eta^{\prime}
. According to
[
MSW17
, Theorem 7.7 (ii)]
, viewed as a curve the law of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
is a chordal
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
as defined in
[
She09
]
. The curve
\eta^{\prime}
is the so-called trunk of
\widetilde{\eta}^{\prime}
. By the target invariance of
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
, if we iterate the above chordal CPI exploration towards
z
and keep track of the chordal
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
’s along the way, we get a radial
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
on
D
from
x
to
z
. From the relation between the
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
exploration tree and
\CLE_{\kappa}
in
[
She09
]
, after finite many iterations we must reach the loop
\eta
at a point
p
, the place where the radial
\SLE_{\kappa}(\kappa-6)
starts exploring
\eta
.
∎

4.5
Proof of Proposition
4.4

Our proof will depend on conformal welding results from
[
ASYZ24
]
.
We first give a decomposition of CLE via the
continuum exploration tree
. See Figure
3
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
D
be a Jordan domain and let
z\in D
,
x\in\partial D
. Let
\widetilde{\eta}
be a radial
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\rho)
curve in
D
from
x
to
z
, with
\rho=\kappa^{\prime}-6
and force point located infinitesimally clockwise from
x
. Let
\sigma
be the time that the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\widetilde{\eta}([0,\sigma])
containing

is bounded;
this is the first time
\widetilde{\eta}
completes a loop around
z
. Consider each connected component

of

such that

intersects both

and
\widetilde{\eta}([0,\sigma])
, and
\widetilde{\eta}
fills some boundary segment of

in the counterclockwise direction. Let

be the first and last times that
\widetilde{\eta}
hits

, and let

be the concatenation of
\widetilde{\eta}|_{[s(U),t(U)]}
with an independent
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
curve in

from

to
\widetilde{\eta}(s(U))
, so

is a loop with counterclockwise orientation. Let
\Gamma_{0}
be the collection of all such
\eta_{U}
. In each connected component of
D\backslash\bigcup_{\eta_{U}\in\Gamma_{0}}\eta_{U}

sample an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, and let
\Gamma
be the union of
\Gamma_{0}
and these
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
s. Then
\Gamma
has the law of
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in
D
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the continuum exploration tree construction of
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
of Sheffield
[
She09
]
.
∎

.

See Figure

.
Cut

has two marked points; its first (resp. second) marked point corresponds to the third (resp. first) marked point of the quantum triangle. Its inner boundary corresponds to the boundary arc of the quantum triangle between its first and second vertices.

4.12
the claim holds.

Remark 4.14
.

Equation (
4.5
)
gives a stronger result than Lemma
4.13
, which we will not need in the present work. We state it here:

Let
M_{\mathrm{ccw}}
be defined as in the above proof of Lemma
4.13
, let
M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}
be the law of a sample from
M_{\mathrm{ccw}}
with
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated foresting, and let
\{M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}(b)\}_{b>0}
be the disintegration of
M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}
with respect to inner generalized boundary length.
For a sample
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
from
\int_{0}^{\infty}M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}(b)\times(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,1}(b)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})\,db
, forget the marked boundary point of
\mathcal{D}
, and let

be the uniform conformal welding of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{D}
. There is a constant

such that the law of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is
C\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
restricted to the event that the outermost loop around the marked bulk point touches the boundary.

Lemma 4.15
.

There exists a measure

with

denotes the law of a sample from

, then the following holds:

Let

. Let
(\widetilde{D},h,z,x)
be an embedding of a sample from

, let
D
be the connected component of
\mathrm{int}(\widetilde{D})
containing

, and independently sample

and

in

,

(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
corresponding to the regions outside and inside

respectively; the conditional law of

, and we denote the conditional law of

. Furthermore, if we condition on

Proof.

As explained in the second claim of

, Proposition 4.3]
(with their parameter

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\mathrm{QT}^{f}(\frac{3\gamma^{2}}{2}-2,2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2},\gamma^{2}-2;\ell+b,\ell)\times\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma;b)\,d\ell}}\right)^{\#}.

Here,

is the law of the forested quantum triangle with weights
W_{1},W_{2},W_{3}
, and

is the law of the quantum triangle whose boundary arc between the vertices of weights
W_{1}

(resp.

) has generalized boundary length

(resp.

).

Thus, we can choose

to be the law of a sample from
\iint_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{QT}^{f}(\frac{3\gamma^{2}}{2}-2,2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2},\gamma^{2}-2;\ell+a,\ell)\,d\ell\,da
conformally welded to itself (and
M_{\mathrm{cw}}
to be the law after forgetting the foresting).
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Let
(\widetilde{D},h,z,w)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)
, let
D
be the connected component of
\mathrm{int}(\widetilde{D})
containing
z
, and independently sample
\widetilde{\eta}
and
\Gamma
in
D
as in Lemma
4.12
. We first prove the analogous statement in this setting. On the event that
\widetilde{\eta}|
closes a counterclockwise loop at time
\sigma
, the desired independence holds by Lemma
4.13
. If instead
\widetilde{\eta}
closes a clockwise loop at time
\sigma
,
by Lemma
4.13
, conditioned on the length of the loop
\ell
, the decorated quantum surface is obtained by conformally welding samples
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})\sim M_{\mathrm{cw}}^{f}(\ell)^{\#}\times(\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma;\ell)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})
. We may then decompose
\mathcal{D}
in exactly the same way by coupling radial
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
with its
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, and checking if the first loop closed is clockwise or counterclockwise, iterating until a loop is closed in the counterclockwise direction. The conclusion is that if
n\geq 0
clockwise loops are closed before the first counterclockwise loop, then conditioned on
n
, the generalized boundary length
\ell_{0}
of
(\widetilde{D},h,z,w)
, and on the lengths
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}
of these clockwise loops, the conditional law of
(\widetilde{D},h,z,w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
agrees with that of the conformal welding of a sample from

\int_{0}^{\infty}\cdots\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}M_{\mathrm{cw}}^{f}(\ell_{i-1},\ell_{i})^{\#}\times M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}(\ell_{n},b)^{\#}}}\right)\prod_{i=0}^{n}d\ell_{i}\,db.

Here,
M_{\mathrm{cw}}^{f}(\ell_{i-1},\ell_{i})^{\#}
denotes the law of a sample from
M_{\mathrm{cw}}^{f}
conditioned to have inner and outer generalized boundary lengths
(\ell_{i-1},\ell_{i})
, and similarly for
M_{\mathrm{ccw}}^{f}(\ell_{n},b)^{\#}
. We conclude that there exists a measure
\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
on
\mathrm{Ann}_{1}\cup\mathrm{Ann}^{\prime}_{1}
such that, if
\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(b)^{\#}
denotes the law of a sample from
\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
with
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
-decorated foresting conditioned on having inner generalized boundary length
b
, then the law of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
conditioned on the length of the loop surrounding the marked bulk point being
b
is
\mathrm{Weld}(\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(b)^{\#},\mathrm{GQD}(b)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})
.

Next, using Propositions
2.33
and
2.34
, an analogous result holds when
\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)
is replaced by
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,1}
(since we can concatenate an independent sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}
to the marked boundary point of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1,1,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma)
). The desired claim then follows by forgetting the boundary point of
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,1}
and weighting by
L^{-1}
where
L
is the generalized boundary length.
∎

5
Quantum annulus

In this section, we introduce the
quantum annulus
and its generalized variant, which will be crucial to our proof of Theorem
1.1
. Throughout this section, we assume
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
.

Fix
a>0
. For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
,
suppose
(D,h,z)
is an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)
. Let
\Gamma
be a
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
D
independent of
h
.
Recall that
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
is the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
. Let
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
be the quantum length of
\eta
.
To ensure the existence of the disintegration of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
over
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
, we check the following fact.

Lemma 5.1
.

For a Borel set
E\subset\mathbbm{R}
with zero Lebesgue measure,
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}\in E]=0
.

Proof.

Let
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the quantum lengths of the outermost loops in a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
, ordered such that
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}>\cdots
. By the explicit law of
(\ell_{i})_{i\geq 1}
from Proposition
4.1
, for each
i>0
we have
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}[\ell_{i}\in E]=0
. Since
\{\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}\in E\}\subset\cup_{i}\{\ell_{i}\in E\}
, we conclude the proof.
∎

Given Lemma
5.1
, we can define the disintegration of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
over
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
, which we denote by

\{\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(\ell):\ell\in(0,\infty)\}
. We now define the quantum annulus.

Definition 5.2

(Quantum annulus)
.

Given
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta,z)
defined right above, let
A_{\eta}
be the non-simply-connected component of
D\setminus\eta
.
For
a,b>0
, let
\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}(a,b)
be the law of the quantum surface
(A,h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
under the measure
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(b)
.
Let
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
be such that

b|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)=\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}(a,b)

(5.1)

We call a sample from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
a
quantum annulus
.

Remark 5.3

(No need to say “for almost every
b
”)
.

Using the general theory of regular conditional probability, for each
a>0
the measure
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
is only well defined for almost every
b>0
.
The ambiguity does not affect any application of this concept in this paper because we will take integrations over
b
; see e.g., Proposition
5.4
below.
On the other hand, one can give an equivalent definition of
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
for all
a,b>0
in terms of the Liouville field on the annulus
[
ARS22
, Theorem 1.4]
2
2

2

[
ARS22
]
builds on the present work but does not use the existence of a canonical definition of
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
for all
b>0
, so there is no circular dependence between the works.

For these reasons, we omit the phrase “for almost every
b
” in statements concerning
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
.

Given a pair of independent samples from
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
and
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)
,
we can uniformly conformally weld them along the boundary component with length
b
to get a loop-decorated quantum surface with one marked point.
We write
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))
for the law of the resulting decorated quantum surface.
Then Proposition
4.2
can be reformulated as follows.

Proposition 5.4
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
. Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))\,db
.

Proof.

From Proposition
4.2
and the definitions of
\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}
and uniform welding, the law of

(D,h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
under
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}(b)
is

\mathrm{Weld}(\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)^{\#})
. By (
5.1
), this measure equals

\mathrm{Weld}(b|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)^{\#})=b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)).\qed.

The reason we consider
\mathrm{QA}
instead of
\widetilde{\mathrm{QA}}
in Definition
5.2
is that it is in some sense more canonical.
In particular, its total measure has the following simple and symmetric form.

Proposition 5.5
.

The total mass of
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
is

|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}.

More strongly, we will see in Proposition
7.5
that
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)=\mathrm{QA}(b,a)
.

When defining the
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
variant of the quantum annulus, which we denote by
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
, there are some subtleties if one follows the same fashion as in Definition
5.2
, as we will explain in Section
5.3
. We use an alternative treatment. Recalling that Proposition
4.4
already fixes the definition of the probability measure
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}
, we simply define
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
by specifying its total mass. Then we obtain the counterpart of Definition
5.2
as a property of
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
.

The rest of the section is organized as follows, we first prove Proposition
5.5
in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
. In Section
5.3
, we treat
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
in detail.

5.1
Reduction of Proposition
5.5
to the setting of Proposition
4.1

In this section, we reduce Proposition
5.5
to the setting of Proposition
4.1
to use the Levy process defined there.
Recall that the setting where
(D,h,\Gamma)
is an embedding of a sample of
\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
for some
a>0
.
Sample a loop
\eta
from the counting measure on
\Gamma
, and let
\mathbb{M}_{a}
be the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta)
.
In other words, consider
((D,h,\Gamma)/{\sim_{\gamma}},\mathbf{n})
sampled from the product measure

(\mathrm{QD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa})\times\mathrm{Count}_{\mathbb{N}}
where
\mathrm{Count}_{\mathbb{N}}
is the counting measure on
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\eta\in\Gamma
be the outermost loop with the
{\mathbf{n}}
th largest quantum length. Then
\mathbb{M}_{a}
is the law of
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
The following proposition is the analog of Proposition
5.4
for
\mathbb{M}_{a}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Under
\mathbb{M}_{a}
, the law of
(D,h,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

\frac{1}{|\mathrm{QD}(a)|}\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}(b))\,db.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a measure on
H^{-1}(\mathbbm{D})
such that the law of
(\mathbbm{D},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}(a)
if
h
is a sample from
\mathbb{M}
.
We write
\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
as the probability measure for
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
\mathbbm{D}
. Write
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma^{o}}(d\eta)
as the counting measure on the set of outermost loops in
\Gamma
. Then we have the following equality on measures:

(1_{E}\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma^{o}}(d\eta))\mu_{h}(d^{2}z)\mathbb{F}(dh)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)=\mu_{h}|_{D_{\eta}}(d^{2}z)\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma^{o}}(d\eta)\mathbb{F}(dh)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma),

(5.2)

where
D_{\eta}
is the simply-connected component of
\mathbbm{D}\backslash\eta
, and
E=\{z\in D_{\eta}\}
.

If
(h,\Gamma,\eta,z)
is sampled according to the left hand side of (
5.2
), then the law of
(\mathbbm{D},h,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}
and
\eta
is the outermost loop around
z
. Therefore we are in the setting of Proposition
5.4
hence the law of
(\mathbbm{D},h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))\,db
.

If
(h,\Gamma,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is sampled from
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma^{o}}(d\eta)\mathbb{F}(dh)\CLE_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
,
then the law of
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
|\mathrm{QD}(a)|\cdot\mathbb{M}_{a}
by the definition of
\mathbb{M}_{a}
. If we then weight by
\mu_{h}(D_{\eta})
and sample a point
z
from
(\mu_{h}|_{D_{\eta}})^{\#}
, then the law of
(h,\Gamma,\eta,z)
is given by the right hand side of (
5.2
), so the law of
(D,h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b))\,db
. Unweighting by
\mu_{h}(D_{\eta})
and forgetting
z
,
we see that the law of
(D,h,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
under
|\mathrm{QD}(a)|\cdot\mathbb{M}_{a}
is
\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QA}(a,b),\mathrm{QD}(b))\,db
.
Dividing by
|\mathrm{QD}(a)|
, we conclude the proof.
∎

Proposition
5.5
immediately follows from its counterpart under
\mathbb{M}_{a}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The law of the quantum length of
\eta
under
\mathbb{M}_{a}
is

\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}1_{b>0}b|\mathrm{QD}(b)|db}{\sqrt{ab}(a+b)|\mathrm{QD}(a)|}\quad\textrm{for constant }{\mathfrak{C}}=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi}.

(5.3)

We now explain how Proposition
5.7
yields Proposition
5.5
and then prove it in Section
5.2

Proof of Proposition
5.5
assuming Proposition
5.7
.

By Proposition
5.6
, the law of
\ell_{h}(\eta)
under
\mathbb{M}_{a}
is
\frac{1}{|\mathrm{QD}(a)|}b|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)||\mathrm{QD}(b)|\,db
. Comparing this to Proposition
5.7
yields
|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|=\frac{\mathfrak{C}}{\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}
.
∎

5.2
Distribution of the loop length: proof of Proposition
5.7

We first reduce Proposition
5.7
to a problem on the Levy process in Proposition
4.1
. Let

{\beta}=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}=\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}.

(5.4)

Let
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
be the probability measure on
càdlàg processes on
[0,\infty)
describing the law of a
{\beta}
-stable Lévy process
with Lévy measure
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
.
Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a sample from
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Let
J:=\{(x,t):t\geq 0\textrm{ and }\zeta_{t}-\zeta_{t^{-}}=x>0\}
be the set of jumps of
\zeta
.
Given
J
, let
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})\in J
be sampled from the counting measure on
J
.
Namely
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is chosen uniformly randomly from
J
similarly as
\eta
from the outermost loops of
\Gamma
.
Let
M^{\beta}
be the law of
(\zeta,{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
, which is an infinite measure.
For each
a>0
, let
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
and
J_{a}=\{(x,t)\in J:t\leq\tau_{-a}\}
.
Let
M^{\beta}_{a}
be the restriction of the measure
M^{\beta}
to the event
\{{\mathbf{t}}\leq\tau_{-a}\}
.
Let
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}=\frac{\tau_{-a}^{-1}}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau_{-a}^{-1}]}M^{\beta}_{a}
, where
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}
is the expectation with respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Then by Proposition
4.1
we have the following.

Lemma 5.8
.

Suppose
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta)
and
\mathbb{M}_{a}
are as in Proposition
5.6
.
Let
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}>\dots
be the quantum lengths of the outermost loops in
\Gamma
and
\ell_{h}(\eta)
be the quantum length of the distinguished loop
\eta
.
Then the joint law of
\{\ell_{i}:i\geq 1\}
and
\ell_{h}(\eta)
under
\mathbb{M}_{a}

equals the joint law of
\{x:(x,t)\in J\}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
under the measure
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
defined right above.

Proof.

Under the measure
\widetilde{M}_{a}^{\beta}
, the jump
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is chosen uniformly from
J_{a}
.
Now Lemma
5.8
follows from Proposition
4.1
and the fact that
\eta
is chosen
uniformly among all outermost loops of
\Gamma
.
∎

Given Lemma
5.8
, Proposition
5.7
immediately follows from the proposition below.

Proposition 5.9
.

The law of
{\mathbf{b}}
under
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
is

\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}1_{b>0}}{(a+b)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{{\beta}+1}db\quad\textrm{for constant }{\mathfrak{C}}=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi}

(5.5)

Proof of Proposition
5.7
given Proposition
5.9
.

By Proposition
2.16
, we have

|\mathrm{QD}(b)|/|\mathrm{QD}(a)|=(a/b)^{\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}+2}=(a/b)^{{\beta}+\frac{3}{2}}.

By Lemma
5.8
and Proposition
5.9
, the
\mathbb{M}_{a}
-law of the quantum length of
\eta
is

\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}}{(a+b)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{{\beta}+1}db=\frac{{\mathfrak{C}}b|\mathrm{QD}(b)|db}{\sqrt{ab}(a+b)|\mathrm{QD}(a)|}\quad\textrm{ with ${\mathfrak{C}}$ in~\eqref{eq:Levy-jump}}.\qed

Figure 5:
Illustration of Lemma
5.10
and
5.11
. The measure
M^{\beta}
is
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
with a uniformly chosen jump
(\mathbf{b},\mathbf{t})
. Conditioned on
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
, removing
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
and concatenating the two pieces of
(\zeta_{t})
before and after
{\mathbf{t}}
gives a sample
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
. The event
\{{\mathbf{t}}<\tau_{-a}\}
for
\zeta
becomes
\{{\mathbf{t}}<\widetilde{\tau}_{-a}\}
for
\widetilde{\zeta}
.

We now prove Proposition
5.9
.
We first use Palm’s Theorem for Poisson point process to give an alternative description of the measure
M^{\beta}
. See Figure
5
for an illustration.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}=\zeta_{t}-1_{t\geq{\mathbf{t}}}{\mathbf{b}}
. Then the
M^{\beta}
-law of
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is
1_{b>0,t>0}b^{-\beta-1}\,db\,dt
.
Conditioning on
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
, the conditional law of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}
under
M^{\beta}
is
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Equivalently, the joint law of
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the product measure
(1_{b>0,t>0}b^{-\beta-1}\,db\,dt)\times\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.

Proof.

By the definition of Levy measure, the jump set
J
of
\zeta
is a Poisson point process on
(0,\infty)^{2}
with intensity measure
1_{x>0,t>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx\,dt
.
Since
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is chosen from the counting measure on
J
, by Palm’s theorem (see e.g.
[
Kal17
, Page 5]
),
the
M^{\beta}
-law of
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is the same as the intensity measure of
J
, which is
1_{b>0,t>0}b^{-\beta-1}\,db\,dt
.
Moreover, conditioning on
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
, the conditional law of
J\setminus\{({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})\}
is given by the original Poisson point process, which is the
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-law of
J
. Note that
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}=(\zeta_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}
is measurable with respect to the jump set
\{(x,t)\in J:t<{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
Therefore, the conditional law of
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}
conditioning on
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
is the
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
-law of
(\zeta_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}
.
Similarly, conditioning on
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}
, the conditional law of
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t+{\mathbf{t}}}-\widetilde{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{t}}})_{t\geq 0}
under
M^{\beta}
is
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
Concatenating
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{0\leq t<{\mathbf{t}}}
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t+{\mathbf{t}}}-\widetilde{\zeta}_{{\mathbf{t}}})_{t\geq 0}
we see that the conditional law of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}
under
M^{\beta}
is
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
.
∎

Proposition
5.9
is an immediate consequence of the following two lemmas.

Lemma 5.11
.

Recall the expectation
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}
for
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
. The law of
{\mathbf{b}}
under
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
in Proposition
5.9
is

\frac{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\tau_{-a}}/{\tau_{-a-b}}}}\right]}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]}b^{-{\beta}-1}1_{b>0}db.

Proof.

We start from
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
under
M^{\beta}
.
Let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}=\zeta_{t}-1_{t\geq{\mathbf{t}}}{\mathbf{b}}
as in Lemma
5.10
.
Let
\widetilde{\tau}_{-\ell}=\inf\{t:\widetilde{\zeta}_{t}=-\ell\}
for each
\ell>0
. Then
\tau_{-a}=\widetilde{\tau}_{-a+{\mathbf{b}}}
. Moreover,
both the events
\{{\mathbf{t}}<\tau_{-a}\}
and
\{{\mathbf{t}}<\widetilde{\tau}_{-a}\}
are the same as
\inf\{\zeta_{t}:t\in(0,{\mathbf{t}})\}>-a
, hence are equal.
Now the measure
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
can be described as

\frac{\tau^{-1}_{-a}}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]}M^{\beta}_{a}=\frac{\tau^{-1}_{-a}1_{{\mathbf{t}}<\tau_{-a}}}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]}M^{\beta}=\frac{\widetilde{\tau}^{-1}_{-a-{\mathbf{b}}}1_{{\mathbf{t}}<\widetilde{\tau}_{-a}}}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]}M^{\beta}.

(5.6)

Integrating out
{\mathbf{t}}
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on the right side of (
5.6
) and using the joint law of
({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}})
and
(\widetilde{\zeta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
from Lemma
5.10
, we see that the
\widetilde{M}^{\beta}_{a}
-law of
{\mathbf{b}}
is
\frac{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\tau_{-a}}/{\tau_{-a-b}}}}\right]}{\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]}b^{-{\beta}-1}1_{b>0}db
as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
(\zeta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be sampled from
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
and
\tau_{-a}=\inf\{t:\zeta_{t}=-a\}
for each
a>0
.
Then

\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\tau_{-a}}/{\tau_{-a-b}}}}\right]=\frac{a}{a+b}\textrm{ for each }a,b>0.

Moreover,
\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-1}^{-1}]=\frac{\pi}{\sin(-\pi\beta)}
.

Proof.

The process
(\tau_{-s})_{s\geq 0}
is a stable subordinator of index
1/\beta
. Since
(\tau_{-a},\tau_{-2a})
has the distribution as
(\tau_{-2a}-\tau_{-a},\tau_{-2a})
, we have
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\tau_{-a}}/{\tau_{-2a}}}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}
. Similary, we have
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\tau_{-a}}/{\tau_{-b}}}}\right]=\frac{a}{a+b}
if
\frac{a}{b}
is rational. By continuity we can extend this to all
a,b>0
. For the second equality, let
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the Levy process with Levy measure
\Pi(dx)=\Gamma(-\beta)^{-1}1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}dx
so that
\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\lambda Y_{t}}]=e^{t\lambda^{\beta}}
. Let
\sigma_{-s}=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}=-s\}
for
s>0
. Then
\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\lambda\sigma_{-s}}]=e^{s\lambda^{1/\beta}}
and
\mathbbm{E}[\sigma_{-1}^{-1}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}[e^{-\lambda\sigma_{-1}}]d\lambda=\Gamma(1+\beta)
.
By scaling, we have
\mathbbm{E}[\tau_{-1}^{-1}]=\Gamma(-\beta)\mathbbm{E}[\sigma^{-1}_{-1}]=\Gamma(-\beta)\Gamma(1+\beta)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(-\pi\beta)}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.9
.

With respect to
\mathbbm{P}^{\beta}
, we have
\tau_{-a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}a^{\beta}\tau_{-1}
by the scaling property of
{\beta}
-stable Lévy processes. Therefore
\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-a}]=\mathbbm{E}^{\beta}[\tau^{-1}_{-1}]a^{-{\beta}}
. Combined with Lemmas
5.11
and
5.12
we get (
5.5
) with
{\mathfrak{C}}=1/\mathbbm{E}^{{\beta}}[\tau^{-1}_{-1}]=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}-1))}{\pi}
.
∎

5.3
Generalized quantum annulus

In this section, we introduce the
generalized quantum annulus
. It is a quantum surface with annulus topology
3
3

3

We allow the random annulus to possibly have pinch points.
.
We assume
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and
\gamma=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\kappa}^{\prime}}\in(\sqrt{2},2)
in this subsection.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
where
D
is a bounded domain. Recall that a loop
surrounds
a point if they are disjoint and the loop has nonzero winding number with respect to the point, and a loop
\eta\in\Gamma
is
outermost
if no point in
\eta
is surrounded by a loop in
\Gamma
. Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
and
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
be the generalized quantum length of
\eta
. To ensure the existence of disintegration of
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
over
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
, we need similar argument as Lemma
5.1
. This is directly from Proposition
4.3
, which describes the joint law of generalized quantum length as the jump size of Lévy process. So the disintegration of
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
over
\mathfrak{l}_{\eta}
exists, which we denoted by
\{\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\ell);\ell\in(0,\infty)\}.

We first try to define the generalized quantum annulus
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
following the same approach as
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)
in Definition
5.2
, then explain that this “definition” requires more care to be correct.

“Definition” 5.13
.

Given
a>0
and
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
defined right above, let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
. Let
D_{\eta}
be the region surrounded by
\eta
and
A_{\eta}=D\setminus D_{\eta}
. For
b>0
, let
\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}(a,b)
be the law of the quantum surface parametrized by
A_{\eta}

under the measure
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(b)
.
Let
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
be such that

b|\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathrm{GA}(a,b)=\widetilde{\mathrm{GA}}(a,b)

(5.7)

Under this “definition” one can obtain the analog of Proposition
5.4
via exactly the same method in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
:

|\mathrm{GA}(a,b)|=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}.

(5.8)

The reason we will not actually use “Definition”
5.13
is that the quantum surface
(A_{\eta},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
has too many self-intersection points. Indeed, in the conformal welding of a generalized quantum annulus and a generalized quantum disk (Proposition
5.15
below), each double point of the interface
\eta
will correspond to a local cut point of exactly one of the generalized quantum surfaces. “Definition”
5.13
can be fixed by removing the self-intersection points of
(A_{\eta},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
which correspond to local cut points of the generalized quantum surface surrounded by
\eta
. This could be done similarly as in
[
MSW21
]
. However,
we instead give an alternative treatment which is simpler to present and follow. What is important for us is that (
5.8
) and Proposition
5.15
(the analog of Proposition
5.4
) both hold for this definition.

Definition 5.14

(Generalized quantum annulus)
.

For
a,b>0
, define

\mathrm{GA}(a,b):=\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}

where
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}
is the probability measure from Proposition
4.4
. We call a sample from
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)
a
generalized quantum annulus
.

Proposition 5.15
.

For
a>0
, let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Let
\eta
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
surrounding
z
.
Then the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals

\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GA}(a,b),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b))\,db
, as well as the law of the decorated quantum surface
(D,h,\Gamma,\eta,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
equals
\int_{0}^{\infty}b\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})\,db
.

Proof.

We only prove the second claim since the first claim will then follow by just forgetting the loop configuration.
By Proposition
4.4
,
conditioned on the length of
\eta
being
b
, the conditional law of
(D,h,\eta,\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)^{\#},\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})
. It thus suffices to show that the law of the length of
\eta
is
b\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}|\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)|\,db
.
Exactly as in Section
5.1
, this reduces to showing that for a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}(a)^{\#}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and an outermost loop chosen from counting measure, the law of the loop length is
\frac{\cos(\pi(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}-1))}{\pi\sqrt{ab}(a+b)}\frac{b|\mathrm{GQD}(b)|}{|\mathrm{GQD}(a)|}\,db
(the analog of Proposition
5.7
), and by Lemma
2.28
this latter measure can be written as
\frac{\cos(\pi(\beta^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2}))}{\pi(a+b)}(\frac{a}{b})^{\beta^{\prime}+1}\,db
where
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}
.

Thus, by Proposition
4.3
, it suffices to prove Proposition
5.9
when the Lévy measure is
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta^{\prime}-1}\,dx
with
\beta^{\prime}=\frac{4}{\kappa^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
rather than
1_{x>0}x^{-\beta-1}\,dx
with
\beta=\frac{4}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}\in(\frac{3}{2},2)
. The proof of Proposition
5.9
directly carries over to the parameter range
(1,\frac{3}{2})
so we are done. ∎

Finally, we give a corrected version of “Definition”
5.13
, where instead of attempting to define the forested boundary by removing the extra self-intersection points of
(A_{\eta},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, we simply forget and resample the forested boundary using the notion of a forested line segment (Definition
2.25
). We will not use this description in the present work.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
(D,h,\Gamma,z)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and let
\eta\in\Gamma
be the outermost loop around
z
. Let
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
be the boundary of the unbounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
, and let
D(\eta^{\mathrm{out}})
be the bounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
. Choose
p\in\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
such that
(D\backslash D(\eta^{\mathrm{out}}),\Gamma,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is measurable with respect to
(D\backslash D(\eta^{\mathrm{out}}),\Gamma,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Conditioned on

(D\backslash D(\eta^{\mathrm{out}}),\Gamma,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, sample a forested line segment
\mathcal{L}
conditioned on having quantum length (resp. generalized boundary length) equal to that of
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
(resp.
\eta
), and in each connected component of
\mathcal{L}
sample an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Glue
\mathcal{L}
to the boundary loop
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
of
(D\backslash D(\eta^{\mathrm{out}}),\Gamma,z,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, identifying the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
with
p
. Let
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}
be the law of the resulting generalized quantum surface, and let
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(a,b)
be the disintegration of
\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}
according to the outer and inner generalized boundary lengths. Then
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}(a,b)=(b\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b))^{-1}\widehat{\mathrm{GA}}^{\rm d}(a,b)
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Proposition
5.15
and the fact that for a forested quantum surface (such as a sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\rm d}
), conditioned on the unforested quantum surface, the foresting is described by a forested line segment with specified quantum length.
∎

6
Equivalence of two definitions of the SLE loop

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
, namely for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
,
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}=C\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
for some
C\in(0,\infty)
, where

\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
is Zhan’s loop measure (Definition
3.1
), and
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
is the loop measure obtained from
choosing a loop in a full-plane
\CLE^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\kappa}
from the counting measure.
For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
, this was already implicitly shown in
[
KW16
]
; see
[
AS21
, Theorem 2.18]
for details.

Recall from Section
1.3
the probability measures
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
that describe the shapes of
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
, respectively. Namely, given a loop
\eta
on
\mathbbm{C}
surrounding 0, we have
R_{\eta}=\inf\{|z|:z\in\eta\}
and
\hat{\eta}=\{z:R_{\eta}z\in\eta\}
.
Suppose
\eta
is a sample from
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
restricted to the event that
\eta
surrounds
0
.
Then the law of
(\hat{\eta},\log R_{\eta})
is a constant multiple of the product measure
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}\times dt
, where
dt
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbbm{R}
.
The same holds for
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
when
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},8)
.
By definition, Theorem
1.1
is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
.
