The main device for the proof of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
is a natural Markov chain with stationary distribution
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
.
We describe it in the cylinder coordinate for convenience.
Consider the horizontal cylinder
\mathcal{C}=\mathbbm{R}\times[0,2\pi]
with
x\in\mathbbm{R}
identified with
x+2\pi i
. We also include
\pm\infty

in
\mathcal{C}
so that
\mathcal{C}
is conformally equivalent to a Riemann sphere.
Let
\psi(z)=e^{-z}
be a conformal map from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathbbm{C}\cup\{\infty\}
with
\psi(+\infty)=0
and
\psi(-\infty)=\infty
.

Definition 6.1
.

Fix
\kappa\in(0,8)
. Let
\eta
be a sample from
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
restricted to the event that
\eta
surrounds
0
, hence
\psi^{-1}(\eta)
is a loop on
\mathcal{C}
separating
\pm\infty
. Let
\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
be the law of
\psi^{-1}(\eta)
. We also write
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}):=(\psi^{-1})_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}):=(\psi^{-1})_{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
.

Let
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
be the set of simple loops
\eta
on
\mathcal{C}
separating
\pm\infty
such that

\max\{\Re z:z\in\eta\}=0
. The relation between
\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is the following.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\eta
be a sample from
\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then
\eta
can be uniquely written as
\eta^{0}+{\mathbf{t}}
where
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbbm{R}
.
The law of
(\eta^{0},{\mathbf{t}})
is
C\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\times dt
for a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
, where
dt
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbbm{R}
.

Proof.

If
\eta
is a sample from
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
restricted to the event that
\eta
surrounds
0
, the law of
(\hat{\eta},\log R_{\eta})
is a constant multiple of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}\times dt
. Therefore Lemma
6.2
follows from mapping
\mathbbm{C}
to
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

In Sections
6.1
–
6.4
we prove Theorem
1.1
for
\kappa\leq 4
; the arguments will mainly focus on
\kappa<4
, while the case
\kappa=4
is obtained by a limiting argument at the end. A proof outline is given in Section
6.1
. In Section
6.5
we carry out the same argument again for
\kappa\in(4,8)
.

6.1
The case
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
: setup and proof overview

The goal of Sections
6.1
–
6.4
is to prove the following.

Proposition 6.3
.

For each
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4]
, there exists
C\in(0,\infty)
such that
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}=C\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
.

Our proof uses the following Markov chain.
By definition,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is a probability measure on
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.
Given
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, let
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta^{0}}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\eta^{0}
containing
+\infty
.
Sample a
\CLE_{\kappa}
on
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta^{0}}
and translate its outermost loop surrounding
+\infty
to an element
\eta^{1}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then
\eta^{0}\rightarrow\eta^{1}
defines a Markov transition kernel on
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.

Recall that the total variational distance between two measures
\mu
and
\nu
on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
is
d_{\operatorname{tv}}(\mu,\nu):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}}|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Fix
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and let
(\eta^{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the Markov chain starting from
\eta^{0}
.
Then the law of
\eta^{n}
tends to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
in total variational distance. In particular,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is the unique stationary probability measure of the Markov chain.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
[
KW16
]
; see Proposition
A.4
for details.
∎

The starting point of our proof of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is the following conformal welding result for
\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.
Fix
a>0
. A pair of quantum surfaces sampled from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\times\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)
can be uniformly conformally welded to get a loop-decorated quantum surface with two marked points (corresponding to the interior marked points of each surface).
We write
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a))
for the law of this loop-decorated quantum surface with two marked points.

Proposition 6.5
.

Fix
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be the law of
h
as in Definition
2.6
, so that the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,+\infty,-\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is the two-pointed quantum sphere
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
.
Now sample
(h,\eta)
from
\mathbb{F}\times\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})

and write
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{sep}}
as the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,\eta,+\infty,-\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Then

\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{sep}}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}a\cdot\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a))\,da\quad\textrm{ for some }C>0.

(6.1)

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
5.6
.
Let
\mathbb{F}_{0}
be a measure on
H^{-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbbm{C})
such that the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is the unmarked quantum sphere
\mathrm{QS}
if
h
is a sample from
\mathbb{F}_{0}
.
Let
(h,\eta,z,w)
be sampled from
\mu_{h}(d^{2}z)\,\mu_{h}(d^{2}w)\,\mathbb{F}_{0}(dh)\SLE^{\mathrm{loop}}_{\kappa}(d\eta)
.
Let
E_{\operatorname{sep}}
be the event that
\eta
separates
z,w
. Then the law of
(h,\eta,z,w)
restricted to
E_{\operatorname{sep}}
can be obtained in two ways:

1.

Sample
(h,z,w)
from
\mu_{h}(d^{2}z)\,\mu_{h}(d^{2}w)\,\mathbb{F}_{0}(dh)
; then sample
\eta
from
\SLE^{\mathrm{loop}}_{\kappa}
on
\mathbbm{C}
and restrict to the event that
\eta
separates
z,w
.

2.

Sample
(h,\eta)
from
2\mu_{h}(D_{\eta})\mu_{h}(D^{\prime}_{\eta})\,\mathbb{F}_{0}(dh)\SLE^{\mathrm{loop}}_{\kappa}(d\eta)
, where
D_{\eta},D_{\eta}^{\prime}
are the connected components of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
; then sample
(z,w)
from the probability measure proportional to

\mu_{h}|_{D_{\eta}}(d^{2}z)\mu_{h}|_{D^{\prime}_{\eta}}(d^{2}w)+\mu_{h}|_{D^{\prime}_{\eta}}(d^{2}z)\mu_{h}|_{D_{\eta}}(d^{2}w).

Recall Theorem
3.2
and the definitions of
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
and
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}
from Section
2.2
.
The law of
(h,\eta,z,w)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
restricted to
E_{\operatorname{sep}}
, from the first sampling, equals
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{sep}}
.
From the second sampling, the same law equals
C\int_{0}^{\infty}a\cdot\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a))\,da
for some
C\in(0,\infty)
.
∎

In the rest of this section we consider the setting where Lemma
6.2
and Proposition
6.5
apply.
For
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
, let
(h,\eta)
be a sample from
\mathbb{F}\times\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then
\eta
can be uniquely written as
\eta^{0}+{\mathbf{t}}
where
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbbm{R}
. By Lemma
6.2
, the law of

(\eta^{0},{\mathbf{t}})
is
C\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})\times dt
.
Conditioning on
(h,\eta)
, sample a
\CLE_{\kappa}

\Gamma^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
is the component of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\eta
containing
+\infty
.
Let
(\eta_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the set of loops in
\Gamma^{+}
separating
\pm\infty
ordered from left to right.
We write
\eta_{i}=\eta^{i}+t_{i}
where
\eta^{i}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
t_{i}\in\mathbbm{R}
.
See Figure
6
for an illustration.

Figure 6:

Left:
Illustration of
\eta_{i}
and
t_{i}
.

Right:
Illustration of
\eta^{i}
.

To prove
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
,
consider the decorated quantum surface
S_{0}=(\mathcal{C},h,\eta,\pm\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
S_{i}=(\mathcal{C},h,\eta_{i},\pm\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
for
i\geq 1
.
If
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
holds, then the stationarity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
gives the stationarity of
(S_{i})_{i\geq 0}
.
Although we cannot show this directly before proving
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
, we will prove using Proposition
5.4

that the law of the subsurface of
S_{i}
on the right side of
\eta_{i}
indeed does not depend on
i
.
This is the content of Section
6.2
.
In Section
6.3
we use Lemma
6.4
to show that as
i\to\infty
the law of
S_{i}

converges to
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\widetilde{\SLE}^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
in a suitable sense, where
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\widetilde{\SLE}^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
is defined as

\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
in Proposition
6.5
with
\widetilde{\SLE}^{\mathrm{loop}}_{\kappa}
in place of

\SLE^{\mathrm{loop}}_{\kappa}
. This convergence would be immediate in the total variational sense if the law of
S_{i}
were a probability measure
instead of being
infinite
. To handle this subtlety we will prove some intermediate results that will also be useful in Section
8
.

Once the two steps in the previous paragraph are achieved, we will have that the right subsurface of
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\widetilde{\SLE}^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}

has the same law as that of
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
. Then by left/right symmetry we can conclude that
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\widetilde{\SLE}^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}=\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
, hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.
We carry out these final steps and complete the proof of Proposition
6.3
in Section
6.3
.
In Section
6.4
we supply a technical ingredient in the proof coming from the fact that
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}
is an infinite measure.

6.2
Stationarity of the subsurfaces on the right side of the loops

Consider
(h,\eta)
and
(\eta^{i})_{i\geq 0}
as defined in and below Proposition
6.5
.
Let
S_{0}=(\mathcal{C},h,\eta,\pm\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
S_{i}=(\mathcal{C},h,\eta_{i},\pm\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
for
i\geq 1
as discussed right above Section
6.2
.
For
i\geq 0
, let
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
be the quantum length of
\eta_{i}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The law of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
is the same as that of
\ell_{h}(\eta)
in Proposition
6.5
for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

By definition the law of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{0})
is the same as that of
\ell_{h}(\eta)
in Proposition
6.5
.
It remains to show that the law of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
does not depends on
i\geq 0
. By Propositions
5.4
and
6.5
,
the joint law of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{0})
and
\ell_{h}(\eta_{1})
is given by

Cab|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)||\mathrm{QA}(a,b)||\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\,da\,db\quad\textrm{for some }C\in(0,\infty).

Since
|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)|=|\mathrm{QA}(b,a)|
by Proposition
5.5
, the laws of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{0})
and
\ell_{h}(\eta_{1})
are the same.
By Proposition
5.4
, and the fact that
\Gamma^{+}
restricted to
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta_{i}}
is still a
\CLE_{\kappa}
,
the conditional law of
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
given
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i-1})
does not depend on
i
.
Since
\ell(\eta_{0})
and
\ell_{h}(\eta_{1})
have the same law, so do
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
and
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i+1})
for all
i\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

For
i\geq 0
, conditioning on
(h,\eta_{i})
, let
z_{i}
be a point on
\eta_{i}
sampled from the probability measure proportional to the quantum length measure.
Let
S^{+}_{i}=(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta_{i}},h,+\infty,z_{i})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{-}_{\eta_{i}}
be the component of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\eta_{i}
containing
-\infty
and
S_{i}^{-}=(\mathcal{C}^{-}_{\eta_{i}},h,-\infty,z_{i})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Then the conditional law of
S_{i}^{+}
given
S_{i}^{-}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta_{i}))^{\#}
for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

When
i=0
, this follows from Theorem
3.2
.
The
i\geq 1
case follows iteratively using Proposition
5.4
.
∎

From Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
, we see that the law of
S_{i}^{+}
is a quantum disk whose law does not depend on
i
.
Moreover, conditional on
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})
,
S^{+}_{i}
and
S^{-}_{i}
are conditionally independent and
S
is obtained from the conformal welding of
S^{+}_{i}
and
S^{-}_{i}
.

6.3
Convergence of
S_{i}
and the proof of Proposition
6.3

We retain the setting in Section
6.2
.
For
z\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\phi^{0}(z)=h(z+{\mathbf{t}})
and
\phi^{i}(z)=h(z+t_{i})
for
i\geq 1
.
By definition,
(\mathcal{C},\phi^{i},\eta^{i})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is an embedding of the decorated quantum surface
S_{i}
for
i\geq 0
.
By Theorem
2.11
, we immediately have the following description of the law of
(\phi^{i},\eta^{i})
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Recall the Markov chain in Lemma
6.4
and the Liouville field
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\gamma,\pm\infty)}
from Definition
2.10
.
Let
P_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. For
i\geq 1
, let
P_{i}
be the distribution of the
i
-th step of this Markov chain on
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
with the initial distribution
P_{0}
.
Then the law of
(\phi^{i},\eta^{i})
is
C\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\gamma,\pm\infty)}\times P_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
, where
C\in(0,\infty)
is a constant that does not depend on
i
.

Proof.

By definition, the law of
(h,\eta^{0},\mathbf{t})
is
\mathbb{F}\times P_{0}\times[C\,dt]
, where
\mathbb{F}
is the law of
h
in Definition
2.6
. By the definition of
P_{i}
and the translation invariance of
[C\,dt]
, the law of
(h,\eta^{i},t_{i})
is
\mathbb{F}\times P_{i}\times[C\,dt]
. Finally, Theorem
2.11
yields the result.
∎

From now on we write the measure
C\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\gamma,\pm\infty)}
in Lemma
6.8
as
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}
for simplicity so that the law of
(\phi^{i},\eta^{i})
is
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}
.

From Lemma
6.4
we know that
d_{\operatorname{tv}}(P_{i},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))\rightarrow 0
.
We would like to say that
d_{\operatorname{tv}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})\rightarrow 0
as well, but since

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}
is an infinite measure we need a truncation. Recall that the quantum length
\ell_{h}(\eta)
is a measurable function of
(\phi^{0},\eta^{0})

[
She16
]
. We write this as
\ell_{h}(\eta)=\ell(\phi^{0},\eta^{0})
.
Then
\ell_{h}(\eta_{i})=\ell(\phi^{i},\eta^{i})
a.s. for each
i\geq 0
. For
\varepsilon>0
, consider the event
E_{\varepsilon}=\{\ell(\phi,\eta)>\varepsilon\}
.
Then by Lemma
6.6
we have

\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}(E_{\varepsilon})=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{0}(E_{\varepsilon})\quad\textrm{ for each }i\geq 0\textrm{ and }\varepsilon>0.

(6.2)

We claim that the total variational convergence holds after restriction to
E_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
,
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{{\operatorname{tv}}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}|_{E_{\varepsilon}},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})|_{E_{\varepsilon}})=0
.

The proof of Lemma
6.9
is not hard but technical so we postpone it to Section
6.4
.
We proceed to finish the proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
given Lemma
6.9
.

Let
(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})
be a sample from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.
Conditioning on
(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})
, let
z\in\widetilde{\eta}

be sampled from the probability measure proportional to the quantum length measure of
\widetilde{\eta}
.
Recall the decorated quantum surfaces
S_{i}^{+}
and
S_{i}^{-}
from Lemma
6.7
.
We similarly define
\widetilde{S}^{+}=(\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\eta}}^{+},\phi,+\infty,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
and
\widetilde{S}^{-}=(\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\eta}}^{-},\phi,-\infty,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\eta}}^{+}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\eta}}^{-}
) is the component of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\widetilde{\eta}
to the right (resp., left) of
\widetilde{\eta}
.
By Lemma
6.9
, restricted to the event
E_{\varepsilon}
, the law of
(S_{i}^{+},S_{i}^{-})
converge in total variational distance to that of
(\widetilde{S}^{+},\widetilde{S}^{-})
.
Since
\varepsilon
can be arbitrary,
by Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
and Equation (
6.2
), the joint law of
\ell(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})

and
\widetilde{S}^{+}
under
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})

is the same as that of
\ell(\phi,\eta)
and
S^{+}_{0}
in Lemma
6.7
.
Moreover, conditioning on
\ell(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})
, the decorated quantum surfaces
\widetilde{S}^{+}
and
\widetilde{S}^{-}
are conditionally independent.

Now we use the additional observation that both
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
are invariant under the mapping
z\mapsto-z
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}
(in the case of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}
we also translate the reflected loop so it lies in
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
).
Therefore
(\widetilde{S}^{+},\widetilde{S}^{-})
must agree in law with
(\widetilde{S}^{-},\widetilde{S}^{+})
. Hence
(\widetilde{S}^{+},\widetilde{S}^{-})
agrees in law with
(S_{0}^{+},\widetilde{S}_{0}^{-})
in Lemma
6.7
.
Namely, if we uniformly conformal weld
\widetilde{S}^{+}
and
\widetilde{S}^{-}
, the resulting decorated quantum surface is
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\operatorname{sep}}
from Proposition
6.5
.

The conditional law of
(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})
given
(\widetilde{S}^{+},\widetilde{S}^{-})
is obtained by conformally welding
\widetilde{S}^{+},\widetilde{S}^{-}
then embedding the decorated quantum surface in
(\mathcal{C},-\infty,+\infty)
in a rotationally invariant way around the axis of the cylinder. The same holds for
(\phi^{0},\eta^{0})
and
(S^{+}_{0},S^{-}_{0})
. Consequently
(\phi,\widetilde{\eta})
and
(\phi^{0},\eta^{0})
agree in distribution, i.e.
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
where
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is the law of
(\phi^{0},\eta^{0})
by Lemma
6.8
.
Therefore
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}=\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
as desired, hence
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}=C\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
for some constant
C
. This proves Proposition
6.3
for
\kappa\in(8/3,4)
. For
\kappa=4
, we can take the
\kappa\uparrow 4
limit as explained in Lemmas
A.5
and
A.6
to get
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{4}=\mathcal{L}_{4}
and conclude the proof.
∎

6.4
Convergence in total variation: proof of Lemma
6.9

We first make a simple observation.

Lemma 6.10
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we have
(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]\leq(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]<\infty
.

Proof.

We consider a coupling
\{\eta^{i}:i\geq 0\}
of
(P_{i})_{i\geq 0}
such that for each
i\geq 1
,
\mathbbm{P}[\eta^{i}\neq\eta^{0}]
achieves the minimum among all couplings of
P_{i}
and
P_{0}
.
Then in this coupling we can find a subsequence
i_{k}
such that
\mathbbm{P}[\eta^{i_{k}}\neq\eta^{0}]\leq 1/k^{2}
hence by Borel-Cantelli lemma
\eta^{i_{k}}=\eta^{0}
a.s. for large enough
k
.
Now we take the product measure of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}
and this coupling. Then by Fatou’s lemma we have

(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i_{k}})[E_{\varepsilon}]
. On the other hand, by Lemma
6.6
,

(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}]=(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{0})[E_{\varepsilon}]=(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]
for all
i\geq 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Given
\eta\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, let
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\eta
containing
+\infty
.
Let
f:\mathcal{C}_{+}\to\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
be a conformal map such that
f(+\infty)=\infty
, where
\mathcal{C}_{+}:=\{z\in\mathcal{C}\>:\>\Re z>0\}
is the half-cylinder.
By standard conformal distortion estimates (e.g.
[
MS19
, Lemma 2.4]
), there exists a positive constant
C_{0}
not depending on
\eta
such that
|f(z)-z|<\frac{C_{0}}{3}
and
|f^{\prime\prime}(z)|<\frac{1}{2}<|f^{\prime}(z)|<2
for
\Re z>\frac{C_{0}}{3}
(these are quantitative versions of the facts that
\mathrm{CR}(\exp(-\eta),0)\approx 1
and
\lim_{z\to+\infty}f^{\prime}(z)=1
).
We need the following estimate for embeddings of
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
on
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
that is uniform in
\eta\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose
\eta\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
\phi
is such that the law of
(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
for some
\ell>0
.
Suppose
g
is a deterministic smooth function on
\mathcal{C}
supported on
\{z\in\mathcal{C}:\Re z\in[C_{0},C_{0}+1]\}
such that
\int g(z)\,d^{2}z=1
.
Then

\mathbbm{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K+\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell,K+\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell)]\rightarrow 0\textrm{ as }K\to\infty

where the convergence rate only depends on
g
, but not on
\eta
,
\ell
or the precise law of
\phi
.

Proof.

If
(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}

then
(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi-\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
. Therefore
it suffices to prove that if the law of
(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
, then

\mathbbm{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K,K)]\rightarrow 0\textrm{ as }K\to\infty

(6.3)

where the convergence rate only depends on
g
.

To prove (
6.3
), let
f:\mathcal{C}_{+}\to\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
be a conformal map such that
f(+\infty)=+\infty
and set
\phi_{0}=\phi\circ f+Q\log|f^{\prime}|
.
Then
(\mathcal{C}_{+},\phi_{0},+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}=(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, hence its law is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
. Let
S
be the collection of smooth functions
\xi
that are supported on
\{\Re z\in[\frac{2}{3}C_{0},\frac{4}{3}C_{0}+1]\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{+}
and satisfy
\|\xi\|_{\infty}\leq 4\|g\|_{\infty}
and
\|\nabla\xi\|_{\infty}\leq 8(\|g\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla g\|_{\infty})
. By the definitions of
C_{0}
and
f
, we see that
|f^{\prime}|^{2}g\circ f\in S
.
Let
Y=\sup_{\xi\in S}|(\phi_{0},\xi)|
. Since
\phi_{0}
is almost surely in the local Sobolev space of index
-1
, we have
Y<\infty
a.s.
Note that
\|f^{\prime}(z)1_{\Re z\in[C_{0},C_{0}+1]}\|_{\infty}\leq 2
so

|(\phi,g)|=|(\phi_{0}\circ f^{-1}+Q\log|(f^{-1})^{\prime}|,g)|\leq|(\phi_{0},|f^{\prime}|^{2}g\circ f)|+Q\log 2\leq Y+Q\log 2.

Since the law of
\phi_{0}
is unique modulo rotations around the axis of
\mathcal{C}
, the law of
Y
does not depend on
\eta
or the precise law of
\phi
.
Therefore,
\mathbbm{P}[(\phi,g)\not\in(-K,K)]\leq\mathbbm{P}[Y+Q\log 2>K]\to 0
as
K\to\infty
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Let
g
be a smooth function on
\mathcal{C}
as in Lemma
6.11
. For
K>0
,
define

S_{K}=E_{\varepsilon}\cap\{(\phi,\eta)\in H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C}):(\phi,g)\in(-K+\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell(\phi,\eta),K+\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\ell(\phi,\eta)\}.

(6.4)

Then
d_{{\operatorname{tv}}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}|_{E_{\varepsilon}},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}|_{E_{\varepsilon}})
is bounded from above by

d_{{\operatorname{tv}}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}|_{S_{K}},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}|_{S_{K}})+(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K}]+(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})[E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K}].

(6.5)

Since
(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})[E_{\varepsilon}]<\infty
by Lemma
6.10
and

\cap_{K>\infty}(E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K})=\emptyset
, we see that

\lim_{K\to\infty}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})[E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K}]=0.

(6.6)

On the other hand, conditioning on the boundary length being
\ell
, the conditional law of
(\mathcal{C}_{\eta^{i}}^{+},\phi^{i},\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
.
Since
\eta^{i}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, by Lemma
6.11
, we have

(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K}]\leq(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}]\times o_{K}(1)

where
o_{K}(1)
is a function converging to
0
as
K\to 0
uniform in
i\geq 0
. Since
(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}]
does not depend on
i
by (
6.2
), we have

\lim_{K\to\infty}\max_{i\geq 0}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i})[E_{\varepsilon}\setminus S_{K}]=0.

(6.7)

It remains to handle the first term in (
6.5
). Let
F_{K}=\{\phi\in H^{-1}(\mathcal{C}):(\phi,g)>-K+\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\varepsilon\}
. Then

S_{K}\subset F_{\varepsilon,K}\times\mathrm{Loop}_{0}
. We claim that
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}[F_{\varepsilon,K}]<\infty
. Assuming this, since
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{\mathrm{tv}}(P_{i},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})=0
, we have
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{\mathrm{tv}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}|_{F_{\varepsilon,K}}\times P_{i},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}|_{F_{\varepsilon,K}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa})=0
hence
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{\mathrm{tv}}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}|_{S_{K}},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}|_{S_{K}})=0
. Thus the quantity (
6.5
) tends to 0 as
i\to\infty
then
K\to\infty
, as desired.

By the definition of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\gamma,\pm\infty)}
from Definition
2.10
, our claim
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}[F_{\varepsilon,K}]<\infty
follows from
the fact that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbbm{P}[G>-c]e^{(2\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc<\infty
if
G
is a Gaussian random variable.
∎

6.5
The
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
case

In this section, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 6.12
.

For each
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, there exists
C\in(0,\infty)
such that
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}=C\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
.

The proof here follows the framework of previous sections line by line, where the equivalence of
\SLE_{\kappa}
loop measure for
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
is proved. Therefore, we just list the main steps for the case
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
and briefly mention the differences with the arguments in the proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Similar to the simple case, we let
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
be the set of non-crossing loops on the cylinder
\mathcal{C}
separating
\pm\infty
with
\max\{\Re z:z\in\eta\}=0
. Define
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
to be the shape measure of Zhan’s
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loop measure
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
(see Definition
3.1
), and define
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
to be the shape measure of
\widetilde{\SLE}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{loop}}
(the counting measures of the full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
). Finally, we say a loop
\eta
in
\mathcal{C}

surrounds

+\infty
if the loop
\exp(-\eta)
in
\mathbbm{C}
surrounds
0
(has nonzero winding number around
0
).

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\eta^{0}}^{+}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{C}\backslash\eta^{0}
that contains
+\infty
. As the simple case, if one samples
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
configuration on
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta^{0}}
, and translates its outermost loop surrounding
+\infty
to be an element
\eta^{1}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, then we define a Markov transition kernel
\eta^{0}\to\eta^{1}
on
\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
. The following lemma gives the
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
counterpart of Lemma
6.4
(also extends the result of Proposition 4 in
[
KW16
]
to the nonsimple case).

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
(\eta^{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the Markov chain starting from
\eta^{0}
. Then
\eta^{n}
converges in the total variation distance to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
is the unique stationary measure of the Markov chain.

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose
0\in D\subset\widetilde{D}
are two Jordan domains in
\mathbbm{C}
. Let
\Gamma
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}
be
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
configurations in
D
and
\widetilde{D}
respectively. Then there is a coupling of
\Gamma
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}
such that with a positive probability
c>0
, their loops surrounding the origin coincide.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{D}
be a domain such that
D\subset\widehat{D}\subset\widetilde{D}
and both of
\partial D\cap\partial\widehat{D}
and
\partial\widetilde{D}\cap\partial\widehat{D}
have connected subsets
\alpha,\beta
more than one point. Let
0\in A\subset D
be such that
\alpha\subset\partial A
and
\partial A
does not intersect
\partial D\Delta\partial\widehat{D}
. Similarly, let
0\in B\subset\widehat{D}
be such that
\beta\subset\partial B
and
\partial B
does not intersect
\partial\widehat{D}\Delta\partial\widetilde{D}
. Fix
a\in\alpha
,
b\in\partial D\backslash\partial A
,
c\in\beta
and
d\in\partial\widetilde{D}\backslash\partial B
. Let
\eta,\widehat{\eta}^{ac},\widehat{\eta}^{ca},\widetilde{\eta}
be the chordal
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
from
a
to
b
in
D
, from
a
to
c
and from
c
to
a
in
\widehat{D}
, and from
c
to
d
in
\widetilde{D}
(with the force points being
a+
,
a+
,
c-
,
c-
respectively, where
+
stands for the couterclockwise direction).

In the following we will couple
\eta
and
\widetilde{\eta}
such that with a positive probability
0
is
disconnected
, i.e. not encircled by
\eta
(resp.
\widetilde{\eta}
) and clockwise boundary arc
\overline{ba}
(resp.
\overline{cd}
) in
D
(resp.
\widetilde{D}
), and the connected components of
D\backslash\eta
and
\widetilde{D}\backslash\widetilde{\eta}
containing
0
are the same.
Once this coupling successes, according to the construction of
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
by
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)
exploration trees in Lemma
4.12
(which is based on
[
She09
]
), when
0
is disconnected in the above sense, the
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
loops surrounding
0
are from the conditionally independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in the remaining connected component containing
0
.

We will establish the coupling by using
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
and
\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
as intermediaries. Note that
\eta
and
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
can be viewed as the counterflow line of GFFs in
D
and
\widehat{D}
with some boundary data, which coincide on
\partial D\cap\partial\widehat{D}\subset A
. By the absolute continuity of GFF (see e.g.
[
MS16a
, Proposition 3.4]
), the law of restriction
h|_{A}
and
\widehat{h}|_{A}
are mutually absolutely continuous. Since the counterflow line is determined by the field
[
MS16a
, Proposition 5.13]
, we find that the laws of
\eta
and
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
before exiting
A
are mutually absolutely continuous.
For a non self-touching curve
\gamma
from
a
and disconnecting
0
, let
\tau
be its first time of that
0
is disconnected, and
\sigma<\tau
be such that
\gamma(\sigma)=\gamma(\tau)
. We call
\gamma[\sigma,\tau]
to be the
disconnecting loop
of
0
. Now define the event
E
to be that
0
is disconnected before exiting
A
, and the disconnecting loop of
0
is in
A\cap B
. Since
E
is measurable w.r.t. the curve before exiting
A
, once
\mathbbm{P}[E]>0
, we can deduce that conditioned on the event
E
, the laws of the connected components of
D\backslash\eta
and
\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
containing
0
are mutually absolutely continuous.
Similarly, we can define the disconnecting loop of
0
for curves from
c
in parallel, and define the event
\widetilde{E}
to be that
0
is disconnected before exiting
B
, and the disconnecting loop of
0
is in
A\cap B
. For the same reason, once
\mathbbm{P}[\widetilde{E}]>0
, we find conditioned on
\widetilde{E}
, the laws of the connected components of
\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
and
\widetilde{D}\backslash\widetilde{\eta}
containing
0
are mutually absolutely continuous.

By the reversibility of the chordal
\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\kappa^{\prime}-6)

[
MS16c
, Theorem 1.2]
,
\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
and the time-reversal of
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
are equal in law, hence in the following we set
\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
to be the time-reversal of
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
. Define the event
\widehat{E}:=\{\widehat{\eta}^{ac}\text{ is in }E\}\cap\{\text{the time-reversal }\widehat{\eta}^{ca}\text{ of }\widehat{\eta}^{ac}\text{ is in }\widetilde{E}\}
. Once
\mathbbm{P}[\widehat{E}]>0
, by the domain Markov property, we find that the laws of
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}[0,\tau]
(
\tau
is the time that
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
disconnects
0
), conditioned on
E
and
\widehat{E}
respectively, are mutually absolutely continuous. Indeed, if
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}[0,\tau]
satisfies the event
E
, then with a positive probability the remaining part of
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
will stay in
B
, which achieves the event
\widehat{E}
. Hence we deduce that conditioned on
E
and
\widehat{E}
, the laws of the connected components containing
0
of
\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
are mutually absolutely continuous. For the same reason, conditioned on
\widetilde{E}
and
\widehat{E}
respectively, the laws of the connected components containing
0
of
\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
are mutually absolutely continuous. Since we have set
\widehat{\eta}^{ca}
to be the time-reversal of
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
, we have
\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ca}=\widehat{D}\backslash\widehat{\eta}^{ac}
. Combining with the previous paragraph, we conclude that the laws of the connected components containing
0
of
D\backslash\eta
and
\widetilde{D}\backslash\widetilde{\eta}
, conditioned on
E
and
\widetilde{E}
respectively, are mutually absolute continuous.
Since then, by the maximal coupling, one can couple
\eta
(restricted on
E
) and
\widetilde{\eta}
(restricted on
\widetilde{E}
) such that the connected components of
D\backslash\eta
and
\widetilde{D}\backslash\widetilde{\eta}
containing
0
coincide with a positive probability.

It remains to show that the probabilities for events
E,\widetilde{E},\widehat{E}
are all positive. In the following we show
\mathbbm{P}[\widehat{E}]>0
; the cases for other two events can be done similarly.
Fix three tubes
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
as the figure below. According to
[
GMQ18
, Lemma A.1]
, the following three (conditional) probabilities are all positive: the event
E_{1}
that
\eta
hits the marked boundary of
T_{2}
before exiting
T_{1}
; conditioned on
E_{1}
, the event
E_{2}
that
\eta
disconnects
0
and then hits
T_{3}
before exiting
T_{2}
; and conditioned on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, the event
E_{3}
that
\eta
ends at
c
without leaving
T_{3}
. Thus
\mathbbm{P}[E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}]>0
. Since
E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}\subset\widehat{E}
, we have
\mathbbm{P}[\widehat{E}]>0
.

∎

Figure 7:
Left
: The configuration that the event
E
occurs, where the orange loop corresponds to the segment
\widehat{\eta}^{ac}[\sigma,\tau]
.
A
and
B
are the yellow and pink regions.
Right
: Three tubes
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
are colored grey light to dark, such that
T_{1}\subset A
,
T_{2}\subset A\cap B
and
T_{3}\subset B
. The marked boundary of
T_{2}
is colored black.

Proof of Lemma
6.13
.

Let
0\in D\subset\mathbbm{C}
be a Jordan domain and consider a full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
configuration
\Gamma
. Denote
\gamma
to be the inner-most loop in
\Gamma
surrounding
D
, and let
G
be the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\gamma
containing
0
(and also
D)
. Conditionally on
\Gamma
, independently take
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
configurations
(\Gamma_{D},\Gamma_{G})
on
D
and
G
from the coupling in Lemma
6.14
, and denote its the success probability to be
c(G)
. Note that the resampling property for full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
gives that the collection of loops
\widetilde{\Gamma}:=\Gamma|_{\mathbbm{C}\backslash G}\cup\{\gamma\}\cup\Gamma_{G}
also has the law of full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
. Hence we obtain the desired coupling
(\Gamma_{D},\widetilde{\Gamma})
for
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
on
D
and
\mathbbm{C}
such that with probability
\mathbbm{E}c(G)
their loops surrounding
0
in
D
coincide (
\mathbbm{E}c(G)>0
since
c(G)>0
a.s.).
∎

The following proposition is the counterpart of Proposition
6.5
. Its proof is just by the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
6.5
, with the input Theorem
3.3
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Fix
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
. For some
C>0
, we have

\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\rm{sep}}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}a\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a),\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a))da.

For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, let
(h,\eta)
be a sample from
\mathbb{F}\times\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then
\eta
can be uniquely written as
\eta^{0}+{\mathbf{t}}
where
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
{\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbbm{R}
. The law of

(\eta^{0},{\mathbf{t}})
is
C\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})\times dt
.
Conditioning on
(h,\eta)
, sample a
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}

\Gamma^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
.
Let
(\eta^{i})_{i\geq 1}
be the set of loops in
\Gamma^{+}
separating
\pm\infty
ordered from left to right (note that
(\eta^{i})_{i\geq 1}
is just the Markov chain with the transition kernel defined above).
Let
\ell_{h}(\eta^{i})
be the quantum length of
\eta^{i}
. The following is the counterpart of Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 6.16
.

The law of
\ell_{h}(\eta^{i})
is the same as the law of
\ell_{h}(\eta^{0})
.

Proof.

By Propositions
5.15
and
6.15

the joint law of
\ell_{h}(\eta^{0})
and
\ell_{h}(\eta^{1})
is

Cab|\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(a)||\mathrm{GA}(a,b)||\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(b)|\,da\,db,

and by Definition
5.14

|\mathrm{GA}(a,b)|=|\mathrm{GA}(b,a)|
so this expression is symmetric in
a
and
b
. Thus the lemma holds for
i=1
, and inductively for general
i
.
∎

We now state Lemma
6.17
, which is the
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
analog of Lemma
6.7
. In Lemma
6.7
we defined quantum surfaces
S_{i}^{+}
and
S_{i}^{-}
parametrized by the regions
C_{\eta_{i}}^{+}
and
C_{\eta_{i}}^{-}
to the right and left of the (simple) loop
\eta_{i}
. In the present setting
\eta_{i}
is nonsimple, so to state Lemma
6.17
, we will define the analogous
forested
quantum surfaces
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{-}
parametrized by regions
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{-}
; these regions have to be defined via
index
with resepct to the loop, rather than “right” and “left”.

For
\eta^{0}\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, we define
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta^{0}}^{+}
as follows. Let
f(z)=e^{-z}
be the conformal map from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathbbm{C}
, and let
D_{\eta^{0}}
be the set of points on
\mathbbm{C}
whose index w.r.t.
f\circ\eta^{0}
is non-zero (in particular,
0\in D_{\eta^{0}}
). We then set
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta^{0}}^{+}=f^{-1}(D_{\eta^{0}})
(so
+\infty\in\mathfrak{C}_{\eta^{0}}^{+}
). One can similarly define
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta^{0}}^{-}
.
By Proposition
6.15
the quantum surfaces parametrized by
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{-}
are forested quantum surfaces when
i=0
, and inductively by Proposition
5.15
they are forested quantum surfaces when
i>0
.

Lemma 6.17
.

For
i\geq 0
, conditioning on
(h,\eta_{i})
, let
z_{i}
be a point on
\eta_{i}
sampled from the probability measure proportional to the generalized quantum length measure. Let
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{+}
(resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{-}
) be the forested quantum surface parametrized by
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{+}
(resp.
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta_{i}}^{-}
) having marked points points
z_{i}
and
+\infty
(resp.
-\infty
). Then the conditional law of
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{+}
given
\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{-}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,1}(\ell_{h}(\eta_{i}))^{\#}
for all
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

When
i=0
, this follows from Theorem
3.3
.
The
i\geq 1
case follows iteratively using Proposition
5.15
.
∎

Let
E_{\varepsilon}
be the event that the generalized quantum length of
\eta
is larger than
\varepsilon
. The following is the counterpart of Lemma
6.9
.

Lemma 6.18
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
,
\lim_{i\to\infty}d_{\rm tv}(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times P_{i}|_{E_{\varepsilon}},\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})|_{E_{\varepsilon}})=0
.

Proof of Proposition
6.12
given Lemma
6.18
.

According to
[
GMQ18
, Theorem 1.1]
, the inversion invariance also holds for
\CLE^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, i.e.

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathcal{C})
is invariant under the mapping
z\mapsto-z
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}
.
Then the result just follows line by line in the proof of Proposition
6.3
, with the
\kappa<4
inputs replaced by Lemmas
6.13
and
6.17
and Proposition
6.15
.
∎

Now it remains to prove Lemma
6.18
. To this end, we repeat the argument in Section
6.4
. The key input is the following counterpart of Lemma
6.11
. Recall the universal constant
C_{0}
defined above Lemma
6.11
.

Lemma 6.19
.

Suppose
\eta\in\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C})
and
\phi
are such that the law of
(\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+},\phi,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
. Suppose
g
is a deterministic smooth function on
[C_{0},C_{0}+1]\times[0,2\pi]
with
\int g(z)d^{2}z=1
. Then

\mathbb{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K+\frac{\gamma}{2}\log\ell,K+\frac{\gamma}{2}\log\ell)]\to 0\text{ as }K\to\infty,

where the convergence rate only depends on
g
.

Proof.

Similarly as in Lemma
6.11
, if the law of
(\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+},\phi,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(\ell)^{\#}
, then the law of
(\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+},\phi-\frac{\gamma}{2}\log\ell,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
. Thus it suffices to show that if the law of
(\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+},\phi,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
, then

\mathbb{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K,K)]\to 0\text{ as }K\to\infty,

(6.8)

where again the convergence rate depends only on
g
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{C}\backslash\eta
containing
+\infty
. Sample a point
z
on
\eta^{\rm in}=\partial\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta}
from the probability measure proportional to quantum length, and let
S^{+}=(\mathcal{C}^{+}_{\eta},\phi,+\infty,z)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Since the law of
(\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+},\phi,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{GQD}_{1,0}(1)^{\#}
, the conditional law of
S^{+}
given the quantum length
\ell_{\phi}(\eta^{\rm in})
is
\mathrm{QD}_{1,1}(\ell_{\phi}(\eta^{\rm in}))^{\#}
. Since
C_{0}>0
, we have
\mathfrak{C}_{\eta}^{+}\cap[C_{0},C_{0}+1]=\mathcal{C}_{\eta}^{+}\cap[C_{0},C_{0}+1]
. Thus, by Lemma
6.11
applied to
\eta^{\mathrm{in}}
,

\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K,K)|\ell_{\phi}(\eta^{\rm in})]=0.

(6.9)

Since

\mathbb{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K,K)]=\mathbbm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{P}[(\phi,g)\notin(-K,K)|\ell_{\phi}(\eta^{\rm in})]}}\right]
, the dominated convergence theorem and (
6.9
) imply (
6.8
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.18
.

Recall
E_{\varepsilon}
is the event that the generalized quantum length of
\eta
is larger than
\varepsilon
. Following line by line as in Lemma
6.10
, one can first show that
(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]\leq(\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C}))[E_{\varepsilon}]<\infty
.
Then the proof is the same as the proof of Lemma
6.9
, except that we need to replace
S_{K}
in (
6.4
) with

S_{K}=E_{\varepsilon}\cap\{(\phi,\eta)\in H^{-1}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathrm{Loop}_{0}(\mathcal{C}):(\phi,g)\in(-K+\frac{\gamma}{2}\log\ell_{\phi}(\eta),K+\frac{\gamma}{2}\log\ell_{\phi}(\eta))\},

and use Lemma
6.19
in the place of Lemma
6.11
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Propositions
6.3
and
6.12
prove the claim for
\kappa\in(0,4]
and
\kappa\in(4,8)
.
∎

7
Applications of equivalence of loop measures

In this section, we present some applications of Theorem
1.1
.
In Section
7.1
, we prove Theorem
1.2
. In Section
7.2
, we combine Theorem
1.1
with the conformal welding results for the SLE loop (Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
) to obtain conformal welding results for CLE; these will be used in our subsequent work
[
ACSW24
]
. Finally, in Section
7.3
we prove that
\mathrm{QA}
and
\mathrm{GA}
are symmetric, in the sense that they are invariant in law under the interchange of their boundary components.

7.1
Duality of the SLE loop measure

In this section we prove Theorem
1.2
: For
\kappa\in(2,4)
and
\eta
sampled from
\SLE^{\rm loop}_{16/\kappa}
on
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}=\mathbbm{C}\cup\{\infty\}
, letting
\eta^{\rm out}
be the boundary of the unbounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\backslash\eta
, the law of
\eta^{\rm out}
equals
C\SLE^{\rm loop}_{\kappa}
for some constant
C
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

In this proof we let
C
be a constant depending only on
\gamma
that may change from line to line.

Let
(\hat{\mathbbm{C}},h,\eta,\infty)
be an embedding of a sample from
\mathrm{QS}_{1}\otimes\SLE_{16/\kappa}^{\rm loop}
, and let
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
be the boundary of the connected component of
\hat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
containing
\infty
. Let
M_{1}
be the law of
(\hat{\mathbbm{C}},h,\eta^{\mathrm{out}},\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
. Let
p,q
be independently sampled from the probability measure proportional to the quantum length measure on
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
, and let
M_{3}
be the law of
(\hat{\mathbbm{C}},h,\eta^{\mathrm{out}},p,q,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
weighted by the square of the quantum length of
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
. We will show that

M_{3}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}_{0,2}(b,a),\mathrm{QD}_{1,2}(a,b))\,da\,db.

(7.1)

Given (
7.1
), by forgetting the points on
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
and deweighting, we get
M_{1}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell),\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(\ell))\,d\ell
, so Theorem
3.2
tells us that
M_{1}=C\mathrm{QS}_{1}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
. The desired result follows. The rest of the proof is devoted to showing (
7.1
).

By Theorem
3.3

\eta
cuts
(\hat{\mathbbm{C}},h,\eta,p,q,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
into a pair of forested quantum surfaces; let
\mathfrak{S}^{+}
be the forested quantum surface containing marked points
(p,q,\infty)
, and let
\mathfrak{S}^{-}
be the other forested quantum surface (having marked points
(p,q)
). Similarly,
\eta^{\mathrm{out}}
cuts
(\hat{\mathbbm{C}},h,\eta^{\mathrm{out}},p,q,\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
into a pair of quantum surfaces
S^{+}
and
S^{-}
, where
S^{+}
has marked points
(p,q,\infty)
and
S^{-}
has marked points
(p,q)
. See Figure
8
.

By Theorem
3.3
and Proposition
2.34
, we have
\mathrm{QS}_{1}\otimes\SLE_{16/\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell),\mathcal{M}_{1,0,0}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma;\ell))\,d\ell
. Thus,
writing
\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(2;\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
to denote the law of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(2;\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
with a bulk point sampled from the probability measure proportional to the quantum area measure on the thick quantum disk (without foresting) and with law weighted by the quantum area of the thick quantum disk (without foresting), by Definition
2.15
, the law of
(\mathfrak{S}^{-},\mathfrak{S}^{+})
is

\iint_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell_{2},\ell_{1})\times\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(2;\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\,d\ell_{1}\,d\ell_{2}
. Recall from Definition
2.27
that
\mathrm{GQD}_{0,2}(\ell_{2},\ell_{1})=\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.d.}}(\gamma^{2}-2;\ell_{2},\ell_{1})
, and that two-pointed forested quantum disks arise from welding forested line segments to two-pointed quantum disks (Definition
2.26
). Thus, the joint law of
(S^{-},S^{+})
is

C\int\mathrm{Weld}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.l.}}(b,\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.l.}}(t_{2},\ell_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma^{2}-2;t_{2},t_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.l.}}(t_{1},\ell_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{f.l.}}(\ell_{1},a)}}\right)\times\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2;a,b)

with integral taken over
a,b,\ell_{1},\ell_{2},t_{1},t_{2}>0
and welding interfaces forgotten. By Proposition
3.6
, the first term in the integrand integrated over
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0
simplifies to
C\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2};b,t_{2}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\gamma^{2}-2;t_{2},t_{1}),\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2};t_{1},a))
, and by Proposition
3.4
integrating this term over
t_{1},t_{2}>0
gives
C\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2;b,a)
. We conclude that the joint law of
(S^{-},S^{+})
is
C\iint_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2;b,a)\times\mathcal{M}_{2,\bullet}^{\mathrm{disk}}(2;a,b)\,da\,db
, so by Definition
2.15
we obtain (
7.1
).
∎

Figure 8:

Left
: Forested quantum surfaces
\mathfrak{S}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{S}^{+}
.
Right
: By Proposition
2.34
,
S^{+}
is a sample from
\mathrm{QD}_{1,2}
. On the other hand, splitting the forested line segments off of
\mathfrak{S}^{\pm}
and welding them together (Proposition
3.6
), we can express
S^{-}
in terms of the conformal welding of two-pointed quantum disks with weights
(2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2},\gamma^{2}-2,2-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2})
, hence it is a two-pointed quantum disk with weight
2
(Proposition
3.4
). Thus we can identify the joint law of
S^{-}
and
S^{+}
.

7.2
Full-plane CLE via conformal welding

Theorem
1.1
yields the following variant of Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a measure on
H^{-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbbm{C})
such that the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QS}

if
h
is sampled from
\mathbb{F}
. For
\kappa\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
, we sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta)
from

\mathbb{F}(dh)\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, where
\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
is the law of a full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa}
and
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)
is the counting measure on a sample
\Gamma
from
\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
. For
\ell>0
, let
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa},\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa})
be the quantum surface decorated by a loop ensemble and a distinct loop obtained from uniformly welding a pair of
\CLE
-decorated quantum disks sampled from
[\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}]\times[\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}]
.
Then the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\cdot\mathrm{Weld}\big(\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa},\mathrm{QD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}\big)\,d\ell\quad\textrm{for some }C\in(0,\infty).

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

This immediately follows from Theorems
1.1
and
3.2
, combined with the following property of full-plane
\CLE^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\kappa}
from
[
KW16
]
.

Proposition 7.2

(
[
KW16
]
)
.

Sample
(\Gamma,\eta)
from
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
. Conditioning on
\eta
, the conditional law of
\Gamma\setminus\{\eta\}
is given by two independent
\CLE_{\kappa}
’s on the two components of
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
. ∎

The analog of Proposition
7.2
holds for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
as well.

Proposition 7.3
.

Sample
(\Gamma,\eta)
from
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
. Conditioning on
\eta
, the conditional law of
\Gamma\setminus\{\eta\}
is given by sampling independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
’s on the each connected components of
\widehat{\mathbbm{C}}\backslash\eta
.

Proof.

From
[
GMQ18
, Lemma 2.9]
, let
\eta_{n}
be the sequence of loops of
\Gamma
surrounding
0
. For each
n
,
\gamma_{n}
splits
\mathbbm{C}
into countably many connected components. If we condition on

\eta_{n}
and the set of loops
in
\Gamma
which are contained in the unbounded connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta_{n}
, then the conditional law
of the rest of
\Gamma
is that of an independent
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
in each bounded connected
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\gamma_{n}
. By conformal invariance of full-plane CLE (
[
GMQ18
, Theorem 1.1]
), we can swap the roles of the connected component containing
0
and the unbounded component, hence the result follows.
∎

We have the following analog of Theorem
7.1
for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a measure on
H^{-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbbm{C})
such that the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathrm{QS}

if
h
is sampled from
\mathbb{F}
. For
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, we sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta)
from

\mathbb{F}(dh)\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
, where
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
is the law of a full-plane
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)
is the counting measure on a sample
\Gamma
from
\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\mathbbm{C}}(d\Gamma)
. For
\ell>0
, let
\mathrm{Weld}(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}},\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}})
be the generalized quantum surface decorated by a loop ensemble and a distinct loop obtained from uniformly welding a pair of CLE-decorated quantum disks sampled from
[\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}]\times[\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}]
.
Then the law of
(\mathbbm{C},h,\Gamma,\eta)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\cdot\mathrm{Weld}\big(\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}},\mathrm{GQD}(\ell)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa^{\prime}}\big)\,d\ell\quad\textrm{for some }C\in(0,\infty).

Proof.

Combine Proposition
7.3
and Theorem
1.1
.
∎

7.3
Symmetry of
\mathrm{QA}
and
\mathrm{GA}

In this section, we show that the quantum annulus and generalized quantum annulus are each invariant in law when their boundaries are interchanged. The proof depends on the invariance in law of
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
under interchange of its marked points, and the inversion invariance of full-plane CLE.

Proposition 7.5
.

For
a,b>0
we have
\mathrm{QA}(a,b)=\mathrm{QA}(b,a)
. In other words, the quantum annulus is invariant in law under the operation of reordering its boundary components.

Proof.

Let
\kappa=\sqrt{\gamma}\in(\frac{8}{3},4)
.
Let
\mathbb{F}
be the law of
h
as in Definition
2.6
, so that the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,+\infty,-\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is the two-pointed quantum sphere
\mathrm{QS}_{2}
. Let
\CLE^{\mathcal{C}}_{\kappa}
denote the law of
\CLE_{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Sample
(h,\Gamma,\eta_{0})
from
\mathbb{F}(dh)\mathrm{Count}_{\Gamma}(d\eta)\CLE^{\mathcal{C}}_{\kappa}(d\Gamma)
and restrict to the event that
\eta_{0}
separates
\pm\infty
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\eta_{0}}^{+}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{C}\backslash\eta_{0}
containing
+\infty
, and let
\eta_{1}
be the outermost loop of
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{C}_{\eta_{0}}^{+}
surrounding
+\infty
.

By Theorem
1.1
, the law of
(h,\eta_{0})
is
C\mathbb{F}\times\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep}}(\mathcal{C})
for some constant
C
, so by Propositions
6.5
and
7.2
the law of
(\mathcal{C}_{\eta_{0}}^{+},h,0,\Gamma|_{\mathcal{C}_{\eta_{0}}^{+}})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
C\int_{0}^{\infty}a|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)\otimes\CLE_{\kappa}\,da
for some constant
C
. Thus, by Proposition
5.4
, writing
A_{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}}
to denote the annular connected component of
\mathcal{C}\backslash(\eta_{0}\cup\eta_{1})
, the law of
(A_{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}},h)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
C\iint_{0}^{\infty}ab|\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(a)||\mathrm{QD}_{1,0}(b)|\mathrm{QA}(a,b)\,da\,db
for some constant
C
.

On the other hand, since for
h\sim\mathbb{F}
the distribution
h(-z)
agrees in law with
h(z)
, and
\Gamma\sim\CLE_{\kappa}^{\mathcal{C}}
is invariant in law under
z\mapsto-z

[
KW16
]
, we conclude that
(A_{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}},h,\eta_{0},\eta_{1})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
agrees in law with
(A_{\eta_{0},\eta_{1}},h,\eta_{1},\eta_{0})/{\sim_{\gamma}}
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

For
a,b>0
we have
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)=\mathrm{GA}(b,a)
. In other words, the generalized quantum annulus is invariant in law under the operation of reordering its boundary components.

Proof.

We first claim that if
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
is a sample from
\mathrm{GA}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
as in Proposition
4.4
, then
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{u.f}}
is invariant in law under the operation of reordering its boundary components. The proof of this claim is identical to that of Proposition
7.5
, except it uses Propositions
6.15
and
7.3
in place of Propositions
6.5
and
7.2
, uses Proposition
5.15
in place of Proposition
5.4
, and uses the inversion invariance of non-simple CLE
[
GMQ18
]
in place of that of simple CLE
[
KW16
]
.

Now, since
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}
is obtained from
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{u.f.}}
by foresting both boundaries and conditioning on boundaries having generalized quantum lengths
a
and
b
, we see that
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)^{\#}=\mathrm{GA}(b,a)^{\#}
. Using Definition
5.14
, we conclude that
\mathrm{GA}(a,b)=\mathrm{GA}(b,a)
.
∎

8
Electrical thickness of the SLE loop via conformal welding

In this section we prove Theorem
1.3
.
Recall from Section
6
the cylinder
\mathcal{C}=\mathbbm{R}\times[0,2\pi]
with
x\in\mathbbm{R}
identified with
x+2\pi i
,
and that
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is the pullback of the loop shape measure
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
under the map
z\mapsto e^{-z}
. Thus
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
is a probability measure on loops
\eta
in
\mathcal{C}
separating
\pm\infty
and satisfying
\max_{z\in\eta}\Re z=0
.
For a loop
\eta
sampled from
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
, we write
\vartheta(\eta)
for the electrical thickness of
\exp(\eta)
.
Then Theorem
1.3
is equivalent to: For
\kappa\in(0,8)

\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\sin(\pi(1-\kappa/4))}{\pi(1-\kappa/4)}\frac{\pi\sqrt{(1-\kappa/4)^{2}+\lambda\kappa/2}}{\sin(\pi\sqrt{(1-\kappa/4)^{2}+\lambda\kappa/2})}&\mbox{if }\lambda<1-\frac{\kappa}{8}.\\
\infty&\mbox{if }\lambda\geq 1-\frac{\kappa}{8}\end{array}}}\right.

(8.1)

Consider
\alpha<Q
and set
\lambda=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}-Q\alpha+2
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
be defined by the following reweighting of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
.

\frac{d\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}}{d\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})}(\eta)=(\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{CR}(\exp(\eta),0)\mathrm{CR}(\exp(-\eta),0))^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}=2^{2\lambda}e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}

(8.2)

Thus proving Theorem
1.3
amounts to computing the total mass
|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|
. We will obtain the following two results by conformal welding of quantum surfaces and forested quantum surfaces:

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,4)
,
\gamma=\sqrt{\kappa}
and
Q=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
. For some constant
C=C(\kappa)
and all
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
we have

2^{-\alpha^{2}+2Q\alpha}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|=C\frac{\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha)}{\sin(\frac{\gamma\pi}{2}(Q-\alpha))}.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
,
\gamma=16/\sqrt{\kappa^{\prime}}
and
Q=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}
. For some constant
C=C(\kappa^{\prime})
and all
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
, we have

2^{-\alpha^{2}+2Q\alpha}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\alpha}|=C\frac{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}{\sin(\frac{2\pi}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}.

Given these, we complete the proof.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

We break the proof of (
8.1
) (hence Theorem
1.3
) into several cases.

Case I:
\kappa<4
and
\lambda\in(1-\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{2}{\kappa},1-\frac{\kappa}{8})
.
Let
\alpha=Q-\sqrt{Q^{2}-4+2\lambda}\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
, so
\lambda=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}-Q\alpha+2
.
By (
8.2
) and Proposition
8.1
we have

\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]=2^{-2\lambda}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|=C\frac{\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha)}{\sin(\frac{\gamma\pi}{2}(Q-\alpha))}=C\frac{\sqrt{(1-\frac{\kappa}{4})^{2}+\frac{\lambda\kappa}{2}}}{\sin(\pi\sqrt{(1-\frac{\kappa}{4})^{2}+\frac{\lambda\kappa}{2}})}

for some constant
C=C(\gamma)
. Since
\kappa\in(0,4)
, we have
0\in(1-\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{2}{\kappa},1-\frac{\kappa}{8})
. Thus we can obtain the value of
C

by considering
1=\mathbbm{E}[e^{0}]=\frac{C(1-\kappa/4)}{\sin(\pi(1-\kappa/4))}
. This yields (
8.1
) in this case.

Case II:
\kappa<4
and
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
.

Since
\vartheta(\eta)\geq 0
a.s. the function
\lambda\mapsto\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]
is increasing. Thus for
\lambda<0
we have
\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]\leq\mathbbm{E}[e^{0\cdot\vartheta(\eta)}]=1
, and since
1-\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{2}{\kappa}<0
we can use analytic continuation to extend the result from
(1-\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{2}{\kappa},1-\frac{\kappa}{8})
to
(-\infty,1-\frac{\kappa}{8})
. Finally, taking a limit from below, we have for any
\lambda\geq 1-\frac{\kappa}{8}
that
\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]\geq\lim_{\lambda^{\prime}\uparrow 1-\frac{\kappa}{8}}\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda^{\prime}\vartheta(\eta)}]=\infty
, where the limit follows from the explicit formula obtained in Case I.

Case III:
\kappa=4
.
For
\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain the result by taking
\kappa\uparrow 4
as follows.
Let
\eta_{\kappa}
be sampled from
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
, then
\vartheta(\eta_{\kappa})\to\vartheta(\eta_{4})
in law as
\kappa\uparrow 4
by Lemma
A.6
.
Fix
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}<1-\frac{4}{8}
. For
\kappa
sufficiently close to
4
the family
\{e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta_{\kappa})}\}
is uniformly integrable, since Theorem
1.3
gives a uniform bound on
\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda^{\prime}\vartheta(\eta_{\kappa})}]
for
\kappa
close to
4
. Therefore
\lim_{\kappa\uparrow 4}\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta_{\kappa})}]=\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta_{4})}]
. Now, for
\lambda\geq\frac{1}{2}
, the monotonicity argument of Case II gives
\mathbbm{E}[e^{\lambda\vartheta(\eta)}]=\infty
.

Case IV:
\kappa\in(4,8)
.
For
\lambda\in(1-\frac{\kappa}{8}-\frac{2}{\kappa},1-\frac{\kappa}{8})
the argument is identical to that of Case I, using Proposition
8.2
instead of Proposition
8.1
. The extension to general
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
follows the same monotonicity argument of Case II.
∎

We now introduce the key objects in our proofs of Propositions
8.1
and
8.2
. Let
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
\kappa\in\{\gamma^{2},16/\gamma^{2}\}\cap(0,8)
(in subsequent sections we will specialize to a fixed value of
\kappa
).

Sample a pair
(\eta,\mathbf{t})
from
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}\times dt
(where
dt
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbbm{R}
), and let
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
be the law of the translated loop
\eta+\mathbf{t}
. Then
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
is an infinite measure on loops on
\mathcal{C}
separating
\pm\infty
.
Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\gamma}=\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
. According to Lemma
6.2
,
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\gamma}
is a constant multiple of the measure
\SLE^{\operatorname{sep}}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
from Section
6
.

Let
\alpha<Q
and
W=2\gamma(Q-\alpha)
. Let
\mathbb{F}
be the law of
h
as in Definition
2.6
,
so that the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,-\infty,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)
. Now sample
(h,\eta)
from

\mathbb{F}\times\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
and write
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
for the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,\eta,-\infty,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.

This object is related to
\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}_{\kappa}
as follows. Recall
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\alpha,\pm\infty)}
on
\mathcal{C}
from Definition
2.10
.

Proposition 8.3
.

If
(\phi,\eta)
is sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\alpha,\pm\infty)}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}
, the law of
(\mathcal{C},\phi,\eta,-\infty,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is

C(Q-\alpha)^{2}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}\quad\text{for some constant }C=C(\kappa)\in(0,\infty).

(8.3)

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the definition of
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
and
[
AHS24
, Theorem 1.2]
, which says the following: let
h
be the field in Definition
2.6
, so the law of
(\mathcal{C},h,-\infty,+\infty)/{\sim}_{\gamma}
is
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)
. Let
T\in\mathbbm{R}
be sampled from Lebesgue measure independently of
h
, and set
\phi:=h(\cdot+T)
. Then
\phi
has law
\frac{\gamma}{4(Q-\alpha)^{2}}\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\alpha,\pm\infty)}
.
∎

In Section
8.1
we will prove Proposition
8.1
(
\kappa<4
). The proof uses a conformal welding result for
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
(Proposition
8.4
) shown in Section
8.2
; the conformal welding gives two different descriptions of
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
, allowing us to compute the size of an event in two different ways, and a computation deferred to Section
8.3
. Next, for
\kappa^{\prime}\in(4,8)
, we review the area statistics of generalized quantum disks with an
\alpha
-insertion in Section
8.4
, and in Section
8.5
we carry out an argument parallel to that of Section
8.1
to prove Proposition
8.2
.

8.1
The case
\kappa\in(0,4)

We now present the conformal welding result needed for the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

Proposition 8.4
.

For
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
and for some constant
C=C(\gamma)
we have

C(Q-\alpha)^{2}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\cdot\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell))\,d\ell.

We postpone the proof of Proposition
8.4
to Section
8.2
and proceed to the proof of Proposition
8.1
.
We would like to compare the area of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}

using Propositions
8.3
and
8.4
to obtain
|\mathcal{L}^{\alpha}_{\kappa}|
and hence prove Theorem
1.3
, but
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}[e^{-A}]=\infty
. We will find a different computable observable that is finite.
Note that
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}
is a measure on quantum surfaces decorated by two marked points and a loop separating them. The loop separates the quantum surface into two connected components. For
0<\varepsilon<\delta
, let
E_{\delta,\varepsilon}
be the event that the connected component containing the first marked point has quantum area at least 1 and the loop has quantum length in
(\varepsilon,\delta)
. The size of
E_{\delta,\varepsilon}
is easy to compute using Proposition
8.4
:

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
. With
C
from Proposition
8.4
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha}[E_{\delta,\varepsilon}]
equals

\frac{1}{C(Q-\alpha)^{2}}\times\frac{(1+o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1))\log\varepsilon^{-1}}{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{-\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)},

where the error term
o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1)
satisfies
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1)=0
.

Proof.

Let
A
be the quantum area of a sample from
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)
.
By Proposition
8.4
, to prove Lemma
8.5
, it suffices to prove

\int_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\ell\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)}}\right|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)[A>1]\,d\ell=\frac{(1+o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1))\log\varepsilon^{-1}}{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{-\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}.

(8.4)

since the left side of (
8.4
) is the mass of
E_{\delta,\varepsilon}
under
\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\cdot\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell),\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell))\,d\ell
.
By the scaling of quantum area and boundary length, we have
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[A>1]=\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;1)^{\#}[A>\ell^{-2}].

By Theorem
2.23
the quantum area law of
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;1)^{\#}
is inverse gamma with shape
a=\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)
and scale
b=(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})^{-1}
. Let
\underline{\Gamma}
be the lower incomplete gamma function; this satisfies
\lim_{y\to 0}\frac{\underline{\Gamma}(a;y)}{y^{a}}=\frac{1}{a}
.
By the tail asymptotic property of the inverse gamma distribution, as
\ell\to 0
,

\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[A>1]=\frac{\underline{\Gamma}(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha);\ell^{2}/4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}=\frac{1+o_{\ell}(1)}{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\ell^{2}}{4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}.

By Proposition
2.18
we have
|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)|=\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-Q)-1}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\ell\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)}}\right|\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)[A>1]\,d\ell=\int_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)\ell^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(\alpha-Q)-1}}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\mathrm{disk}}(\alpha;\ell)^{\#}[A>1]

\displaystyle=\frac{1+o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1)}{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{-\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\ell^{-1}\,d\ell.\qed

We can also compute the size of
E_{\delta,\varepsilon}
using Proposition
8.3
in terms of
|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|
and
\overline{R}(\alpha)
.

Proposition 8.6
.

For
\alpha\in(\frac{\gamma}{2},Q)
, with the error term in the same sense as in Lemma
8.5
,

(\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(W)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\alpha})[E_{\delta,\varepsilon}]=(1+o_{\delta,\varepsilon}(1))\frac{\overline{R}(\alpha)}{2(Q-\alpha)^{2}}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|\log\varepsilon^{-1}.

We postpone the proof of Proposition
8.6
to Section
8.3
and proceed to the proof of Proposition
8.1

using Lemma
8.5
and Proposition
8.6
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

In this proof, we write
C
for a
\kappa
-dependent constant that may change from line to line.
By Lemma
8.5
and Proposition
8.6
we get

(Q-\alpha)^{2}\frac{\overline{R}(\alpha)}{2(Q-\alpha)^{2}}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|=\frac{C}{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)\Gamma(\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}2^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}}\overline{U}(\alpha)}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sin\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4}}}\right)^{-\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}.

Using the definitions of
\overline{R}(\alpha)
and
\overline{U}(\alpha)
in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) and cancelling equal terms gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi\Gamma(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q))}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha))}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4})^{2}\sin(\frac{\pi\gamma^{2}}{4})}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}(Q-\alpha)}C2^{\alpha^{2}-2Q\alpha}\Gamma(1+\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q))^{2}

The identity
\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}
gives equality of the first terms on the left and right hand sides, so rearranging and using
\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)
and
\Gamma(1-z)\Gamma(z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}
gives the desired identity:

2^{-\alpha^{2}+2Q\alpha}|\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}^{\alpha}|=C\frac{\Gamma(1+\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q))}{\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q))}\Gamma(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha))\Gamma(\frac{\gamma}{2}(Q-\alpha))=C\frac{\gamma}{2}(\alpha-Q)\cdot\frac{\pi}{\sin(\frac{\gamma\pi}{2}(Q-\alpha))}.

∎

We now proceed to prove Propositions
8.4
and
8.6
in Sections
8.2
and
8.3
, respectively.

8.2
Conformal welding of two quantum disks with generic insertions

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
8.4
. We start from the
\gamma
-insertion case.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
(\phi,\eta)
is sampled form
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{C}}^{(\gamma,\pm\infty)}\times\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(\mathcal{C})
, then the law of
(\mathcal{C},\phi,\eta,-\infty,+\infty)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
is
C\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell\cdot\mathrm{Weld}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{1,0}(\gamma;\ell),\mathcal{M}^{\rm disk}_{1,0}(\gamma;\ell))d\ell
.

Proof.

Recall
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep}}
from Proposition
6.5
,
which is
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{loop}}
restricted to loops separating
0
and
\infty
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}
, for some
\gamma
-dependent constant
C^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
, the pull back of
\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep}}
via the map
z\mapsto e^{-z}
is

C^{\prime}\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep},\gamma}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{QS}_{2}\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep}}=C^{\prime}\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\mathrm{sph}}(\gamma)\otimes\SLE_{\kappa}^{\mathrm{sep,\gamma}}
.
Now Propositions
8.3
and
6.5
yield Lemma
8.7
.
∎

By the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{disk}}_{1}(\alpha;\ell)
, if we sample a field
X
from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell)
, then the law of
(\mathbbm{H},X,i)/{\sim_{\gamma}}

is
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{disk}}_{1}(\alpha;\ell)
.
We now recall a fact from
[
ARS23
]
about the reweighting of
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)
by “
e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X}
”.

Lemma 8.8

(
[
ARS23
, Lemma 4.6]
)
.

For any
\ell>0,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and for any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
X
that depends only on
X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)}
, we have

\int f(X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)})\times\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\,d{\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\gamma,i)}(\ell)}=\int f(X|_{\mathbbm{H}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(i)})\,d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{H}}^{(\alpha,i)}(\ell),

where
X
is a sample in
H^{-1}(\mathbbm{H})
and
X_{\varepsilon}(i)
means the average of
X
on the boundary of the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(i)=\{z:|z-i|<\varepsilon\}
.

We now state a version for the sphere; the proof is very similar to that of Lemma
8.8
but we include it here for completeness.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\eta_{1}
be a simple loop in
\mathbbm{C}
separating
0
and
1
.
Let
D_{\eta_{1}}
be the connected component of
\mathbbm{C}\setminus\eta_{1}
containing
0
.
Let
p
be a point on
\eta_{1}
and let
\psi:\mathbbm{H}\to D_{\eta_{1}}
be the conformal map with
\psi(i)=0
and
\psi(0)=p
.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, let
\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}=\mathbbm{C}\setminus\psi(B_{\varepsilon}(i))
.
For
\phi
sampled from
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,0),(\gamma,1)}
,
let
X=\phi\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|
so that
(\mathbbm{H},X,i,0)/{\sim_{\gamma}}=(D_{\eta_{1}},\phi,0,p)/{\sim_{\gamma}}
.
Then for a fixed
\alpha\in(Q-\frac{\gamma}{4},Q)
and
for any nonnegative measurable function
f
of
\phi
that depends only on
\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}}
we have

\displaystyle\int f(\phi)\times\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{2}-\gamma^{2})}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}\,d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,0),(\gamma,1)}=\int f(\phi)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+Q\alpha-2}d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,0),(\gamma,1)}.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{\varepsilon}
be the uniform probability measure on
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(i)
and
\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon}:=\psi_{*}\theta_{\varepsilon}
. Recall notations in Section
2.1
, where
\mathbbm{P}_{\mathbbm{C}}
is the probability measure for the GFF on
\mathbbm{C}
. Let
\mathbbm{E}
be the expectation for
\mathbbm{P}_{\mathbbm{C}}
, then
G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z,w)=\mathbbm{E}[h(x)h(y)]=-\log|z-w|+\log|z|_{+}+\log|w|_{+}
. For a sample
h
from
\mathbbm{P}_{\mathbbm{C}}
, we set
\widetilde{h}:=h-2Q\log|\cdot|_{+}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\gamma G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,z_{j})
. Lemma
8.9
will follow from three identities.

e^{(\alpha-\gamma)X_{\varepsilon}(i)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}}}\right)^{(\alpha-\gamma)Q}e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(\phi,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}.

(8.5)

\int f(\phi)\times e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(\phi,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}\,d\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,0),(\gamma,1)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}}}\right)^{-(\alpha-\gamma)\gamma}\int\mathbbm{E}[e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}f(\widetilde{h}+c)]e^{(\alpha+\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc.

(8.6)

\int\mathbbm{E}[e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}f(\widetilde{h}+c)]e^{(\alpha+\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha-\gamma)^{2}}\int f(\phi)\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\alpha,0),(\gamma,1)}.

(8.7)

To prove (
8.5
), note that
X_{\varepsilon}(i)=(X,\theta_{\varepsilon})=(\phi\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|,\theta_{\varepsilon})
. Since
\psi^{\prime}
is holomorphic and
\log|\psi^{\prime}|
is harmonic, we have
(\log|\psi^{\prime}|,\theta_{\varepsilon})=\log|\psi^{\prime}(i)|
hence
(X,\theta_{\varepsilon})=(\phi,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}(i)|
. Since
|\psi^{\prime}(i)|=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)
, we get (
8.5
).

To prove (
8.6
), let
\eta^{\varepsilon}:=\psi(\partial B_{\varepsilon}(i))
.
Since
\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon}
is the harmonic measure on
\eta^{\varepsilon}
viewed from
0
, we have
(\log|\cdot|,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=\log\mathrm{CR}(\eta^{\varepsilon},0)=\log\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}
.
Since
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4}
, the curve
\eta^{\varepsilon}
is contained in the unit disk hence
(\log|\cdot|_{+},\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=0
. Let
G_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\varepsilon}(0,z):=(G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,z),\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})
. Then

G_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\varepsilon}(0,z)=G_{\mathbbm{C}}(0,z)
for
z\in\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}
and
G_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\varepsilon}(0,0)=-\log\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}
.
Thus,

(-2Q\log|\cdot|_{+}+\gamma G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,0)+\gamma G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,1),\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=-\gamma\log\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}
. Therefore
(\widetilde{h}+c,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})-\gamma\log\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}+c
.
Recall from Definition
2.4
that
\mathrm{LF}_{\mathbbm{C}}^{(\gamma,0),(\gamma,1)}
is the law of
\widetilde{h}+c
under
e^{(2\gamma-2Q)c}dc\mathbbm{P}_{\mathbbm{C}}(dh)
. This gives (
8.6
).

To prove (
8.7
), note that
\mathbbm{E}[h(z)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})]=(G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,z),\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=G_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\varepsilon}(0,z)
, which equals
G_{\mathbbm{C}}(z,0)
for
z\in\mathbbm{C}_{\eta_{1},p,\varepsilon}
.
By Girsanov’s theorem and the fact that
f(\phi)
depends only on
\phi|_{\mathbbm{C}_{\eta,p,\varepsilon}}
, we have

\displaystyle\int\mathbbm{E}[e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}f(\widetilde{h}+c)]e^{(\alpha+\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}[e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}]\int\mathbbm{E}[f(\widetilde{h}+(\alpha-\gamma)G^{\varepsilon}_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,0)+c)]e^{(\alpha+\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}[e^{(\alpha-\gamma)(h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})}]\int\mathbbm{E}[f(\widetilde{h}+(\alpha-\gamma)G_{\mathbbm{C}}(\cdot,0)+c)]e^{(\alpha+\gamma-2Q)c}\,dc.

Since
\Var((h,\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon}))=(G_{\mathbbm{C}}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0),\widehat{\theta}_{\varepsilon})=-\log\frac{\varepsilon\mathrm{CR}(\eta_{1},0)}{2}
, we have
